Stationary Vlasov-Poisson-Boltzmann system in a convex domain
Hongxu Chen, Jiaxin Jin

Abstract
. We study the stationary and dynamical Vlasov-Poisson-Boltzmann system in a bounded, convex domain subject to a confining external potential field. For the stationary problem, we construct a unique stationary solution with an inflow boundary condition. A key difficulty is to obtain pointwise regularity for stationary solutions due to the intricate coupling between the self-consistent electric field and the Boltzmann collision operator. To overcome this issue, we establish a W x , v 1 , p W^{1,p}_{x,v} – α ​ C x , v 1 \alpha C^{1}_{x,v} bootstrap framework and derive an unweighted C v 1 C^{1}_{v} estimate by exploiting the structure of the external potential field. We then investigate the dynamical Vlasov-Poisson-Boltzmann system near the stationary solution. We prove the global existence and uniqueness of solutions for small perturbations and establish exponential convergence toward the stationary state in weighted L ∞ L^{\infty} norms. Our results reveal the stabilizing effect of the external potential field and provide a framework for the stationary and dynamical theories of the Vlasov-Poisson-Boltzmann system in bounded domains.



1.
Introduction

The Vlasov–Poisson–Boltzmann (VPB) system is a fundamental kinetic model describing the evolution of dilute charged particles under self-consistent electrostatic interactions and binary collisions
[
11
,
18
,
19
,
24
,
34
,
3
]
.
It plays a central role in plasma physics and kinetic theory, combining two main mechanisms: nonlinear Boltzmann collisions and self-consistent electrostatic forces governed by the Poisson equation.

In this paper, we investigate the stationary VPB system in a three-dimensional convex and bounded domain
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
. The stationary Vlasov-Boltzmann equation is formulated as

(1.1)

v\cdot\nabla_{x}F_{s}-E\cdot\nabla_{v}F_{s}=Q(F_{s},F_{s}),\quad(x,v)\in\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3}.

Here,
F_{s}=F_{s}(x,v)\geq 0
stands for the stationary velocity distribution function of the charged particles with velocity
v=(v_{1},v_{2},v_{3})\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
at position
x\in\Omega
, the boundary conditions are to be specified later.
E
stands for the field. The Boltzmann collision term
Q
is a bilinear integral operator acting only on the velocity variable, and in this manuscript, we only consider the hard sphere model, reading as

Q(F,G):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}|(v-u)\cdot\omega|[F(u^{\prime})G(v^{\prime})-F(u)G(v)]\,\mathrm{d}\omega\mathrm{d}u,

where the velocity pairs
(v,u)
and
(v^{\prime},u^{\prime})
are given in the
\omega
-representation by

v^{\prime}=v+[(u-v)\cdot\omega]\omega,\quad u^{\prime}=u-[(u-v)\cdot\omega]\omega,\quad\omega\in\mathbb{S}^{2},

which corresponds to conservation of momentum and energy for elastic collisions between molecules:

v+u=v^{\prime}+u^{\prime},\quad|v|^{2}+|u|^{2}=|v^{\prime}|^{2}+|u^{\prime}|^{2}.

It is well known that the equilibrium state of the Boltzmann equation is given by Maxwellians, which we denote by

\mu(v):=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{3/2}}e^{-\frac{|v|^{2}}{2}}.

The mathematical theory of kinetic equations of Boltzmann and Vlasov type has been extensively developed. In the whole space or torus setting, global perturbative theories near Maxwellians have been established through high-order energy methods, velocity weights, and hypocoercivity structures, including the Boltzmann equation, VPB system, and Vlasov-Maxwell-Boltzmann system
[
22
,
20
,
35
,
13
,
21
]
.
In bounded domains, however, the presence of boundaries introduces additional difficulties. The transport dynamics interact with boundary conditions (inflow, specular reflection, or diffuse reflection), leading to possible loss of regularity near grazing trajectories. To resolve this issue, a low-regularity space has to be considered for well-posedness. In
[
23
]
, Guo proposed the
L^{2}-L^{\infty}
framework to construct a unique solution to the Boltzmann equation that converges to the equilibrium exponentially fast, with several boundary conditions. Later, Esposito-Guo-Kim-Marra applied the
L^{2}-L^{\infty}
argument and constructed a unique stationary Boltzmann solution with dynamical stability under non-isothermal boundary in
[
15
,
16
]
. We refer to
[
4
,
31
,
12
]
for further developments under different boundary conditions.

In the bounded domain, the regularity issue remains unknown in the
L^{2}-L^{\infty}
framework. In fact, the geometry of the domain plays a crucial role, for instance, in the non-convex domain, singularity may propagates into the interior domain through characteristics, leading to discontinuity, thus BV regularity becomes the optimal regularity as demonstrated in
[
30
,
25
]
. Even in a convex domain, the regularity issue is challenging due to the mixing of the singularity along the characteristic and the velocities interchange induced by the nonlocal Boltzmann operator. A major breakthrough was achieved by Guo-Kim-Tonon-Trescases in
[
26
]
, where a kinetic weight was proposed to compensate the singularity, and the weight
C^{1}
estimate was achieved under several boundary condition in a strictly convex domain. This work inspired further developments in the regularity theory for stationary problems, including the linear problem studied in
[
9
,
8
]
, and the nonlinear problem with higher
C^{1,\beta}
regularity and Sobolev regularity in
[
5
,
6
]
. The stationary regularity estimate provides important methodology for the investigation of the stationary VPB system in the current manuscript. We also mentioned recent progress on regularity in non-convex domains with specular boundary conditions in
[
32
,
1
]
.

For the dynamical VPB system in bounded domains, a recent advancement was achieved by Cao-Kim-Lee in
[
3
]
, who constructed a unique global-in-time solution near equilibrium in convex domains with diffuse boundary conditions.
In particular, they derived weighted
W^{1,p}
regularity for
p<6
to establish the existence and uniqueness. Additional studies on the VPB system under different boundary conditions and potential settings can be found in
[
7
,
33
]
. To refine the weighted
W^{1,p}
regularity, Cao imposed an external potential field with a favorable sign condition on the boundary in
[
2
]
, and constructed a local solution with the weighted
C^{1}
regularity. This external potential field can enhance the regularity by preventing characteristics from becoming tangential to the boundary. A similar type of external field was adopted for the Vlasov–Poisson system in the earlier work
[
27
]
. We further note the dominant gravity field considered in
[
29
,
28
]
also exhibits a similar regularity-enhancing effect.

Despite these developments, the well-posedness of the stationary VPB system remains an open problem. The stationary problem is more challenging than the time-dependent one. In time-dependent settings, time decay provides a natural mechanism to control nonlinear growth, while the Gronwall-type argument can be applied to study the regularity estimate and the stability estimate, such as in
[
3
]
. Moreover, the global well-posedness can be justified through a combination of global a priori estimate and local well-posedness argument. In contrast, stationary systems lack time dissipation and Gronwall’s inequality, and one must rely on structural coercivity, elliptic coupling through the Poisson equation, and precise control of the Vlasov operator to construct the regularity and well-posedness. These difficulties are particularly pronounced in three-dimensional domains, which are also the most relevant setting for physical applications.

In this manuscript, we consider the stationary Vlasov–Poisson–Boltzmann system in a three-dimensional, strictly convex, bounded domain with an external potential field. As a first attempt at the stationary VPB problem, we focus on inflow boundary conditions in this work, while leaving the diffuse reflection boundary condition for future work. The main results of this paper are as follows:

(1)

Construction of a unique stationary solution near the Maxwellian equilibrium.

(2)

Dynamical stability of the stationary solution.

In the remainder of this introduction, we present the basic setting of the problem, state the main results for both the stationary and dynamical systems, and discuss the main difficulties and proof strategy.
Finally, we provide an outline of the paper and introduce the notation used throughout the paper.

1.1.
Basic setting

The three-dimensional domain
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
is assumed to be strictly convex, and bounded with
C^{3}
compact boundary
\partial\Omega
. Below, we provide some geometric descriptions. We fix a sufficiently small parameter
0<\delta_{1}\ll 1
.
We then choose a finite number of points
p\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}\subset\partial\Omega
and
0<\delta_{2}\ll 1
such that

\mathcal{O}_{p}=\eta_{p}(B_{+}(0;\delta_{1}))\subset B(p;\delta_{2})\cap\bar{\Omega},

and
\{\mathcal{O}_{p}\}
forms a finite covering of
\partial\Omega
.
We further choose an interior covering
\mathcal{O}_{0}\subset\Omega
such that
\{\mathcal{O}_{p}\}_{p\in\mathcal{P}}
with
\mathcal{P}=\tilde{\mathcal{P}}\cup\{0\}
forms an open covering of
\bar{\Omega}
.
We define a partition of unity

\mathbf{1}_{\bar{\Omega}}(x)=\sum_{p\in\mathcal{P}}\iota_{p}(x)\ \text{ such that }\ 0\leq\iota_{p}(x)\leq 1,\ \ \iota_{p}(x)\equiv 0\ \text{ for }\ x\notin\mathcal{O}_{p}.

Without loss of generality (see
[
31
]
) we can always reparametrize
\eta_{p}
such that
\partial_{\mathbf{x}_{p,i}}\eta_{p}\neq 0
for
i=1,2,3
at
\mathbf{x}_{p,3}=0
, and an
orthogonality
holds as

\partial_{\mathbf{x}_{p,i}}\eta_{p}\cdot\partial_{\mathbf{x}_{p,j}}\eta_{p}=0\ \text{ at }\ \mathbf{x}_{p,3}=0,\quad i,j\in\{1,2,3\}\text{ and }i\neq j.

And at
\mathbf{x}_{p,3}=0
, the
\mathbf{x}_{p,3}
derivative gives the outward normal:

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}n_{p}(\mathbf{x}_{p})=\frac{\partial_{\mathbf{x}_{p,3}}\eta_{p}}{\langle\partial_{\mathbf{x}_{p,3}}\eta_{p},\partial_{\mathbf{x}_{p,3}}\eta_{p}\rangle}.}

For simplicity, we denote

\partial_{i}\eta_{p}(\mathbf{x}_{p}):=\partial_{\mathbf{x}_{p,i}}\eta_{p}.

For
x\in\partial\Omega
, we choose
p\in\mathcal{P}
such that
x\in\mathcal{O}_{p}
. We denote

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}_{p}:=(\mathbf{x}_{p,1},\mathbf{x}_{p,2},0)\ \text{ such that }\ \eta_{p}(\mathbf{x_{p}})=x.

The tangential derivatives on the boundary can be interpreted by the new coordinates:

\displaystyle\partial_{\mathbf{x}_{p}}f(x,v):=(\partial_{\mathbf{x}_{p,1}}f(x,v),\partial_{\mathbf{x}_{p,2}}f(x,v)),\ x\in\partial\Omega,

\displaystyle\partial_{\mathbf{x}_{p,i}}[f(x,v)]=\partial_{\mathbf{x}_{p,i}}[f(\eta_{p}(\mathbf{x}_{p}),{v})]:=\frac{\partial\eta_{p}(\mathbf{x}_{p,i})}{\partial\mathbf{x}_{p,i}}\cdot\nabla_{x}f(\eta_{p}(\mathbf{x}_{p}),v),\quad i=1,2,\ \ x\in\partial\Omega.

Next, since
\Omega
is strictly convex with
C^{3}
boundary
\partial\Omega
, there exists a
C^{3}
function
\xi(x)
such that

(1.2)

\Omega=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\mid\xi(x)<0\}\ \text{ and }\ \partial\Omega=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\mid\xi(x)=0\}.

On the boundary
\partial\Omega
, we have
|\nabla_{x}\xi(x)|>0
for all
x\in\partial\Omega
, and the outward normal vector is given by

n(x)=\frac{\nabla_{x}\xi(x)}{|\nabla_{x}\xi(x)|},\quad x\in\partial\Omega.

Moreover, the strict convexity of
\Omega
ensures that there exists
C_{\xi}>0
such that for any
x\in\partial\Omega
,

(1.3)

y^{\intercal}\cdot\nabla_{x}^{2}\xi(x)\cdot y\geq C_{\xi}|y|^{2},\quad y\in\mathbb{R}^{3}.

We refer to the construction of such a defining function
\xi(x)
in
[
16
]
. In particular, for some sufficiently small
\delta\ll 1
, we have

(1.4)

\xi(x)=-dist(x,\partial\Omega)\ \text{ when }\ dist(x,\partial\Omega)<\delta.

Let
d>0
. We define the boundary layer region
\Omega_{d}\subset\Omega
by

(1.5)

\Omega_{d}:=\{x\in\Omega\mid dist(x,\partial\Omega)<d\}.

Lemma 1

(
[
2
]
)
.

Suppose that
\partial\Omega
is
C^{2}
. Then there exists a sufficiently small constant
0<\delta=\delta(\Omega)\ll 1
such that for any
x\in\Omega_{\delta}
, there exists a unique
\tilde{x}\in\partial\Omega
satisfying that

dist(x,\tilde{x})=dist(x,\partial\Omega)\ \text{ and }\ \sup_{\Omega_{\delta}}|\nabla_{x}\tilde{x}|<\infty.

Remark 1
.

Lemma
1
implies that each point
x\in\Omega
sufficiently close to the boundary can be uniquely associated with its closest boundary point
\tilde{x}\in\partial\Omega
.
This structure plays a key role in the kinetic weight (see Definition
2
), which is used to control the behavior of characteristics near grazing trajectories.

In a bounded domain
\Omega
, charged particles interact with the physical boundary
\partial\Omega
. We now introduce the boundary structure of the phase space. Let
n(x)
denote the outward normal vector at
x\in\partial\Omega
. We split the boundary phase space
\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}
into the outgoing set
\gamma_{+}
, incoming set
\gamma_{-}
and grazing set
\gamma_{0}
by

\displaystyle\gamma_{+}:=

\displaystyle\{(x,v)\in\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3}:\ n(x)\cdot v>0\},

\displaystyle\gamma_{-}:=

\displaystyle\{(x,v)\in\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3}:\ n(x)\cdot v<0\},

\displaystyle\gamma_{0}:=

\displaystyle\{(x,v)\in\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3}:\ n(x)\cdot v=0\}.

1.2.
Stationary problem and well-posedness

We consider the stationary Vlasov–Poisson–Boltzmann system in the phase space
(x,v)\in\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
, where
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
is the strictly convex and bounded domain introduced in Section
1.1
, in the presence of an external potential field
\phi_{E}
. By substituting
E
in (
1.1
) with the self-consistent electric field and the external potential field, the problem is formulated as

(1.6)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}&v\cdot\nabla_{x}F_{s}-\nabla_{x}(\phi_{F_{s}}+\phi_{E})\cdot\nabla_{v}F_{s}=Q(F_{s},F_{s}),\\
&F_{s}|_{\gamma_{-}}=F_{b},\\
&-\Delta_{x}\phi_{F_{s}}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}F_{s}\mathrm{d}v-\rho_{0}e^{-\phi_{E}}\text{ in }\Omega,\quad\phi_{F_{s}}=0\text{ on }\partial\Omega,\\
&-\partial_{n}\phi_{E}>C_{E}>0\text{ on }\partial\Omega.\end{cases}

Here,
\nabla_{x}\phi_{F_{s}}
represents the self-consistent electric field generated from the stationary charged particles. It can be determined by solving the Poisson equation with the zero Dirichlet boundary condition. In the Poisson equation,
\rho_{0}
represents the background density, and we assume the doping profile is given by
\rho_{0}e^{-\phi_{E}}
under the influence of the external potential
\phi_{E}
.
Without loss of generality, we normalize
\rho_{0}\equiv 1
. Moreover, for the external potential
\phi_{E}
, we impose the favorable sign condition
-\partial_{n}\phi_{E}>C_{E}>0
on the boundary.

With the extra
\phi_{E}
, the equilibrium state of (
1.6
) is given by

F_{s}=e^{-\phi_{E}}\mu\ \text{ when }F_{b}=e^{-\phi_{E}}\mu.

To construct a non-trivial stationary profile, we choose a non-trivial inflow data
F_{b}
, which is a small perturbation around
e^{-\phi_{E}}\mu
, given by

F_{b}=e^{-\phi_{E}}\mu+e^{-\phi_{E}/2}\sqrt{\mu}f_{b}.

Accordingly, we seek a stationary solution of the form

F_{s}=e^{-\phi_{E}}\mu+e^{-\phi_{E}/2}\sqrt{\mu}h.

Substituting this ansatz into the system, the equations for the perturbation
h
are given by

(1.7)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}&v\cdot\nabla_{x}h-\nabla_{x}(\phi_{h}+\phi_{E})\cdot\nabla_{v}h+\frac{v\cdot\nabla_{x}\phi_{h}}{2}h+e^{-\phi_{E}}\mathcal{L}h\\
&\ \ \ \ =-(v\cdot\nabla_{x}\phi_{h})e^{-\phi_{E}/2}\sqrt{\mu}+e^{-\phi_{E}/2}\Gamma(h,h),\\
&h(x,v)|_{\gamma_{-}}=f_{b},\\
&-\Delta_{x}\phi_{h}=e^{-\phi_{E}/2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}h\sqrt{\mu}\mathrm{d}v\text{ in }\Omega,\quad\phi_{h}=0\text{ on }\partial\Omega,\\
&-\partial_{n}\phi_{E}>C_{E}>0\text{ on }\partial\Omega.\end{cases}

Here, the linearized collision operator
\mathcal{L}
and the nonlinear collision operator
\Gamma
are defined by

(1.8)

\mathcal{L}f:=\frac{Q(\mu,\sqrt{\mu}f)+Q(\sqrt{\mu}f,\mu)}{\sqrt{\mu}}\ \text{ and }\ \Gamma(f,f):=\frac{Q(\sqrt{\mu}f,\sqrt{\mu}f)}{\sqrt{\mu}}.

The boundary perturbation is defined as

(1.9)

f_{b}:=\frac{e^{\phi_{E}/2}F_{b}-e^{-\phi_{E}/2}\mu}{\sqrt{\mu}}\ \text{ and }\ \phi_{h}=\phi_{F_{s}}.

In the following, we provide a precise definition of weak solutions to the stationary problem.

Definition 1
.

(a) We say that
(h,\nabla_{x}\phi_{h})\in\big(L^{2}_{loc}(\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3})\;\cap\;L^{2}_{loc}(\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3};\mathrm{d}\gamma)\big)\times L^{2}_{loc}(\Omega)
satisfies the weak formulation of the kinetic equation in (
1.7
), if all terms are bounded and the following condition holds for any test function
\psi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\bar{\Omega}\times\mathbb{R}^{3})
,

(1.10)

\begin{split}&\int_{\gamma_{+}}h(x,v)\psi(x,v)\mathrm{d}\gamma-\int_{\gamma_{-}}f_{b}(x,v)\psi(x,v)\mathrm{d}\gamma-\iint_{\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}h(x,v)v\cdot\nabla_{x}\psi(x,v)\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}x\\
&\ \ \ \ +\iint_{\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}h(x,v)\nabla_{x}(\phi_{h}+\phi_{E})\cdot\nabla_{v}\psi(x,v)\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}x+\iint_{\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\frac{v\cdot\nabla_{x}\phi_{h}}{2}h\psi(x,v)\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}x\\
&\ \ \ \ +\iint_{\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}e^{-\phi_{E}}\mathcal{L}h\psi(x,v)\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}x\\
&=\iint_{\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}-(v\cdot\nabla_{x}\phi_{h})e^{-\phi_{E}/2}\sqrt{\mu}\psi(x,v)\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}x+\iint_{\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}e^{-\phi_{E}/2}\Gamma(h,h)\psi(x,v)\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}x.\end{split}

(b) We say that
(h,\nabla_{x}\phi_{h})\in L^{2}_{loc}(\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3})\times W^{1,2}_{loc}(\Omega)
satisfies the weak formulation of the Poisson equation in (
1.7
), if all terms below are bounded and the following condition holds for any test function
\varphi\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)\cap C^{\infty}_{c}(\bar{\Omega})
,

(1.11)

\int_{\Omega}\nabla_{x}\phi_{h}\cdot\nabla_{x}\varphi\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\Omega}e^{-\phi_{E}/2}\big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}h\sqrt{\mu}\mathrm{d}v\big)\varphi\mathrm{d}x.

Next, we introduce the kinetic and velocity weights for the stationary problem.

Definition 2
.

(a)
Since the potential field is time-independent in the stationary problem (
1.7
), we denote

\displaystyle E_{h}(x)=-\nabla_{x}\big(\phi_{h}(x)+\phi_{E}(x)\big).

Recall
\xi(x)
and
\Omega_{\delta}
introduced in (
1.4
) and (
1.5
), respectively.
By Lemma
1
, for sufficiently small
0<\delta=\delta(\Omega)\ll 1
, each
x\in\Omega_{\delta}
admits a unique
\tilde{x}\in\partial\Omega
such that
dist(x,\tilde{x})=dist(x,\partial\Omega)=-\xi(x)
.
To compensate for the boundary singularity, we choose
0<\delta^{\prime}\ll\delta^{1/2}
and define the kinetic weight
\alpha_{h}(x,v)
by

(1.12)

\begin{split}&\alpha_{h}(x,v)\\
&:=\begin{cases}\chi_{\delta^{\prime}}\Big(\Big[|v\cdot\nabla_{x}\xi(x)|^{2}+\xi^{2}(x)-2(v\cdot\nabla^{2}\xi(x)\cdot v)\xi(x)-2(E_{h}(\tilde{x})\cdot\nabla_{x}\xi(\tilde{x}))\xi(x)\Big]^{1/2}\Big),&\text{if }x\in\Omega_{\delta},\\[5.0pt]
\delta^{\prime},&\text{if }x\in\Omega\setminus\Omega_{\delta}.\end{cases}\end{split}

Here
\chi_{\delta^{\prime}}
is an increasing cut-off function satisfying

(1.13)

\chi_{\delta^{\prime}}(x)=\begin{cases}x,&0\leq x\leq\frac{\delta^{\prime}}{4},\\[5.0pt]
\delta^{\prime},&x\geq\frac{\delta^{\prime}}{2},\end{cases}\quad\text{and}\quad 0\leq\chi_{\delta^{\prime}}^{\prime}(x)\leq 4.

(b) We define the velocity weights
w(v),w_{\tilde{\theta}}(v)
by

\begin{split}&w(v):=e^{\theta|v|^{2}},\ 0<\theta<\frac{1}{4},\ \text{ and }\ w_{\tilde{\theta}}(v):=e^{\tilde{\theta}|v|^{2}},\ 0<\tilde{\theta}\ll\theta.\end{split}

We now state our main result on the existence, uniqueness, and regularity of stationary solutions.

Theorem 1
.

Consider the kinetic and velocity weights
\alpha_{h}(x,v)
,
w(v)
, and
w_{\tilde{\theta}}(v)
defined in Definition
2
.
There exist sufficiently small constants
\delta_{1}\ll\delta_{0}\ll 1
such that, if the external field and inflow data
F_{b}=e^{-\phi_{E}}\mu+e^{-\phi_{E}/2}\sqrt{\mu}f_{b}\geq 0
satisfy

(1.14)

\displaystyle\textbf{(Inflow condition)}

\displaystyle\begin{cases}&\frac{\delta_{0}}{2}\leq C_{E}\leq\|\nabla_{x}\phi_{E}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}<\delta_{0},\\[5.0pt]
&\|e^{-\phi_{E}/2}-1\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}+\|\nabla_{x}^{2}\phi_{E}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}+\|\nabla_{x}^{3}\phi_{E}\|_{L^{p}_{x}}<\delta_{0},\\[5.0pt]
&|wf_{b}|_{L^{\infty}_{\partial\Omega,v}}+|w\nabla_{v}f_{b}|_{L^{\infty}_{\partial\Omega,v}}+|w\partial_{\mathbf{x}_{p}}f_{b}|_{L^{\infty}_{\partial\Omega,v}}<\delta_{1}.\end{cases}

Then there exists a unique solution
F_{s}=e^{-\phi_{E}}\mu+e^{-\phi_{E}/2}\sqrt{\mu}h\geq 0
to (
1.6
).
Moreover, for
2<p<3
and some constants
C_{0},C_{1}>1
, it holds

\displaystyle\|wh\|_{L^{\infty}_{x,v}}<C_{0}\delta_{1}\ll 1,

(1.15)

\displaystyle\|w_{\tilde{\theta}}\alpha_{h}\partial_{x,v}h\|_{L^{\infty}_{x,v}}+\|w_{\tilde{\theta}}\nabla_{v}h\|_{L^{\infty}_{x,v}}+\|w_{\tilde{\theta}}\partial_{x,v}h\|_{L^{p}_{x,v}}<C_{1}\delta_{1}\ll 1.

Theorem
1
is restated as Theorem
17
in Section
7.3
, where its proof is presented.
The positivity of the solution is established in the proof of Theorem
2
in Section
8.4
; see also Remark
4
for further discussion.

Remark 2
.

The velocity regularity is crucial in proving the uniqueness.
For the stationary problem, due to the absence of a Gronwall-type argument, uniqueness needs to be proved under the
L^{2}_{x,v}
space; see the detailed discussion in Section
1.4
.
More precisely, let
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
be two solutions. In the
L^{2}
energy estimate for
h_{1}-h_{2}
, one needs to control the term

\iint_{\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}|\nabla_{v}h_{1}\nabla_{x}(\phi_{h_{1}}-\phi_{h_{2}})||h_{1}-h_{2}|\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}v.

In the three-dimensional case, one needs to place

\nabla_{x}(\phi_{h_{1}}-\phi_{h_{2}})
in
L^{6}_{x}
and apply the Sobolev embedding

\|\nabla_{x}(\phi_{h_{1}}-\phi_{h_{2}})\|_{L^{6}_{x}}\lesssim\|\nabla_{x}(\phi_{h_{1}}-\phi_{h_{2}})\|_{H^{1}_{x}}\lesssim\|h_{1}-h_{2}\|_{L^{2}_{x,v}}.

Consequently, one is led to require the regularity

\|\nabla_{v}h_{1}\|_{L^{3}_{x}L^{2}_{v}}
, which lies beyond the range of the Sobolev regularity

W^{1,p}_{x,v}
with
p<3
.
Therefore, it becomes necessary to introduce the kinetic weight in order to derive higher regularity estimates, such as the point-wise regularity in (
1.15
).

In contrast, for a two-dimensional domain, this difficulty can be overcome by a refined Sobolev embedding. For instance,

\|\nabla_{x}(\phi_{h_{1}}-\phi_{h_{2}})\|_{L^{8}_{x}}\lesssim\|\nabla_{x}(\phi_{h_{1}}-\phi_{h_{2}})\|_{H^{1}_{x}}\lesssim\|h_{1}-h_{2}\|_{L^{2}_{x,v}}.

As a result, the weaker regularity

\|\nabla_{v}h_{1}\|_{L^{8/3}_{x}L^{2}_{v}}
is sufficient, and hence the Sobolev regularity

W^{1,p}_{x,v}
with
p<3
already suffices. Nevertheless, without higher regularity, one cannot expect dynamical stability or positivity. Therefore, the
C^{1}_{v}
regularity remains necessary; see Remark
5
for further discussion.

Remark 3
.

Without the external field, the weighted
C^{1}
regularity in (
1.15
) is not expected in general, as discussed in
[
3
]
.
Indeed, the variance of the kinetic weight

\alpha_{h}^{2}\sim|n(x)\cdot v|^{2}+\mathrm{dist}(x,\partial\Omega)|v|^{2}

along the characteristic becomes singular in the small-velocity regime
|v|\ll 1
due to the electric field. In the presence of an external potential field,
[
2
]
incorporated the distance information and introduced the weight (
1.12
), thereby establishing local well-posedness with weighted
C^{1}
regularity.

In this paper, we adapt this weight and further construct a unique stationary solution with weighted
C^{1}
regularity. This regularity is particularly delicate in the stationary setting, and
W^{1,p}
estimates are required to implement the bootstrap argument; see the detailed discussion in Section
1.4
.
In addition, we establish
C^{1}_{v}
regularity without kinetic weight, which plays an essential role in proving dynamical stability and deriving uniform-in-time estimates; see also Remark
5
for further discussion.

Remark 4
.

The positivity of the stationary solution
F_{s}\geq 0
follows from the positivity and asymptotic stability of the solution
F(t,x,v)
to the dynamical problem. More precisely,

F(t)\geq 0,\qquad\|F(t)-F_{s}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x,v}}\to 0\ \text{ as }t\to\infty,

as established in Theorem
2
.
We refer to the proof of Theorem
2
in Section
8.4
for further details.

1.3.
Dynamical problem and stability analysis

The dynamical problem is given by

(1.16)

\begin{cases}&\partial_{t}F+v\cdot\nabla_{x}F-\nabla_{x}(\phi_{F}+\phi_{E})\cdot\nabla_{v}F=Q(F,F),\\
&F(0,x,v)=F_{0}(x,v),\quad F|_{\gamma_{-}}=F_{b},\\
&-\Delta_{x}\phi_{F}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}F\mathrm{d}v-e^{-\phi_{E}}\text{ in }\Omega,\quad\phi_{F}=0\text{ on }\partial\Omega,\\
&-\partial_{n}\phi_{E}>C_{E}>0\text{ on }\partial\Omega.\end{cases}

To study the dynamical stability around the stationary solution
F_{s}
, we decompose

F=F_{s}+e^{-\phi_{E}/2}\sqrt{\mu}f=e^{-\phi_{E}}\mu+e^{-\phi_{E}/2}\sqrt{\mu}(h+f),

and obtain the equations for
f
:

(1.17)

\begin{cases}&\partial_{t}f+v\cdot\nabla_{x}f-\nabla_{x}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\cdot\nabla_{v}f+\frac{v\cdot\nabla_{x}\phi_{f}}{2}f+e^{-\phi_{E}}\mathcal{L}f\\
&=-e^{-\phi_{E}/2}v\cdot\nabla_{x}(\phi_{f}-\phi_{h})\sqrt{\mu}-\frac{v\cdot\nabla_{x}(\phi_{f}-\phi_{h})}{2}h+\nabla_{x}(\phi_{f}-\phi_{h})\cdot\nabla_{v}h\\
&\ \ \ \ +e^{-\phi_{E}/2}[\Gamma(f,f)+\Gamma(f,h)+\Gamma(h,f)],\\
&f(0,x,v)=f_{0}(x,v):=\frac{F_{0}(x,v)-F_{s}}{e^{-\phi_{E}/2}\sqrt{\mu}},\quad f|_{\gamma_{-}}=0,\\
&-\Delta_{x}\phi_{f}=e^{-\phi_{E}/2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}(h+f)\sqrt{\mu}\mathrm{d}v\text{ in }\Omega,\quad\phi_{f}=0\text{ on }\partial\Omega,\\
&-\partial_{n}\phi_{E}>C_{E}>0\text{ on }\partial\Omega.\end{cases}

Next, we provide a precise definition of weak solutions to the dynamical problem.

Definition 3
.

Suppose
(h,\nabla_{x}\phi_{h})
is a weak solution of (
1.7
) in the sense of Definition
1
.

(a) We say that
(f,\nabla_{x}\phi_{f})\in\big(L^{2}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3})\;\cap\;L^{2}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3};\mathrm{d}\gamma)\big)\times L^{2}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3})
satisfies the weak formulation of the kinetic equation in (
1.17
), if all terms below are bounded and the following condition holds for any test function
\psi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\bar{\Omega}\times\mathbb{R}^{3})
,

\begin{split}&\iint_{\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}f_{0}(x,v)\psi(0,x,v)\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}x-\iint_{\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}f(t,x,v)\partial_{t}\psi(t,x,v)\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t+\iint_{\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}e^{-\phi_{E}}\mathcal{L}f\psi(t,x,v)\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t\\
&+\iint_{\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\gamma_{+}}f(t,x,v)\psi(t,x,v)\mathrm{d}\gamma\mathrm{d}t-\iint_{\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}f(t,x,v)v\cdot\nabla_{x}\psi(t,x,v)\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t\\
&+\iint_{\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}f(t,x,v)\nabla_{x}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\cdot\nabla_{v}\psi(t,x,v)\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t+\iint_{\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\frac{v\cdot\nabla_{x}\phi_{f}}{2}f\psi(t,x,v)\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t\\
&\\
&=\iint_{\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}-(v\cdot\nabla_{x}(\phi_{f}-\phi_{h}))e^{-\phi_{E}/2}\sqrt{\mu}\psi(t,x,v)\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t-\iint_{\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\frac{v\cdot\nabla_{x}(\phi_{f}-\phi_{h})}{2}h\psi(t,x,v)\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t\\
&\ \ \ \ -\iint_{\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\nabla_{x}(\phi_{f}-\phi_{h})\cdot h\nabla_{v}\psi(t,x,v)\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t\\
&\ \ \ \ +\iint_{\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}e^{-\phi_{E}/2}[\Gamma(f,f)+\Gamma(f,h)+\Gamma(h,f)]\psi(t,x,v)\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t.\end{split}

(b) We say that
(f,\nabla_{x}\phi_{f})\in L^{2}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3})\times W^{1,2}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\Omega)
satisfies the weak formulation of the Poisson equation in (
1.17
), if all terms below are bounded and the following condition holds for any
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
and test function
\varphi\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)\cap C^{\infty}_{c}(\bar{\Omega})
,

\int_{\Omega}\nabla_{x}\phi_{f}(t,x)\cdot\nabla_{x}\varphi(x)\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\Omega}e^{-\phi_{E}/2}\big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}(h+f)\sqrt{\mu}\mathrm{d}v\big)\varphi\mathrm{d}x.

For the dynamical problem, the self-consistent electric field depends on time. We denote

E_{f}(t,x):=-[\nabla_{x}\phi_{f}(t,x)+\nabla_{x}\phi_{E}(x)].

The kinetic weight for the dynamical problem is denoted as

(1.18)

\begin{split}&\alpha_{f}(t,x,v)\\
&:=\begin{cases}\chi_{\delta^{\prime}}\Big(\Big[|v\cdot\nabla_{x}\xi(x)|^{2}+\xi^{2}(x)-2(v\cdot\nabla^{2}\xi(x)\cdot v)\xi(x)-2(E_{f}(t,\tilde{x})\cdot\nabla_{x}\xi(\tilde{x}))\xi(x)\Big]^{1/2}\Big),&\text{if }x\in\Omega_{\delta},\\[5.0pt]
\delta^{\prime},&\text{if }x\in\Omega\setminus\Omega_{\delta}.\end{cases}\end{split}

Theorem 2
.

Assume that all assumptions in Theorem
1
hold, and let
F_{s}
be the stationary solution to (
1.6
).
There exists a constant
\delta_{2}\ll 1
such that, if the initial data
F_{0}=F_{s}+e^{-\phi_{E}/2}\sqrt{\mu}f_{0}\geq 0
satisfies the compatibility condition
f_{0}|_{\gamma_{-}}=0
and the smallness condition

(1.19)

\|wf_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x,v}}+\|w_{\tilde{\theta}}\alpha_{f_{0}}\partial_{x,v}f_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x,v}}+\|w_{\tilde{\theta}}\partial_{x,v}f_{0}\|_{L^{p}_{x,v}}+\|w_{\tilde{\theta}}\nabla_{v}f_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x,v}}<\delta_{2},

then there exists a unique solution
F(t)=F_{s}+e^{-\phi_{E}/2}\sqrt{\mu}f(t)\geq 0
to (
1.16
).
Moreover, there exist constants
\lambda\ll 1
and
C_{2},C_{3}>1
such that for any
2<p<3
and
t\geq 0
,

(1.20)

\displaystyle\|e^{\lambda t}wf(t)\|_{L^{\infty}_{x,v}}<C_{2}\delta_{2}\ll 1,

(1.21)

\displaystyle\|w_{\tilde{\theta}}\alpha_{f}\partial_{x,v}f(t)\|_{L^{\infty}_{x,v}}+\|w_{\tilde{\theta}}\partial_{x,v}f(t)\|_{L^{p}_{x,v}}+\|w_{\tilde{\theta}}\nabla_{v}f(t)\|_{L^{\infty}_{x,v}}<C_{3}(\delta_{2}+\delta_{1})\ll 1,

where
\delta_{1}
is the constant defined in Theorem
1
.

Remark 5
.

For the pure Boltzmann equation, the regularity is shown to decay exponentially fast in
[
6
]
. However, in the presence of the additional Vlasov–Poisson coupling, the term
\nabla_{v}h
remains on the right-hand side of the perturbation equation (
1.17
). Due to the lack of control of second-order velocity derivatives of
h
, it is necessary to adopt an alternative formulation.
In particular, we rewrite the solution
F(t,x,v)
as

F(t,x,v)=e^{-\phi_{E}}\mu+e^{-\phi_{E}/2}\sqrt{\mu}\mathfrak{f}.

This yields a reformulated system (
8.17
) in which the term involving
\nabla_{v}h
is replaced by derivatives of the Maxwellian
\nabla_{v}\sqrt{\mu}
.
On the other hand, this formulation leads to a non-homogeneous boundary condition

\mathfrak{f}(t,x,v)|{\gamma_{-}}=f_{b},

and therefore decay in time for
\partial_{x,v}\mathfrak{f}
cannot be expected. Consequently, the dynamical regularity result in (
1.21
) is only uniformly bounded in time, rather than decaying exponentially.

In addition, due to the presence of the term
\nabla_{v}h
on the right-hand side, a
C^{1}_{v}
estimate
\|\nabla_{v}h\|_{L^{\infty}_{x,v}}
is required when applying the
L^{2}
–
L^{\infty}
framework to prove dynamical stability. For the pure Boltzmann equation, the velocity derivative typically exhibits a singularity of order
1/|v|^{2}
, as shown in
[
5
]
. Such a
C^{1}_{v}
estimate can only be recovered in the presence of the external potential field
\nabla_{x}\phi_{E}
. A similar type of
C^{1}_{v}
estimate was also used in
[
29
]
, where a dominant gravitational field is considered for the Vlasov–Poisson system. For a different type of stabilizing effect via an external electric field in the Vlasov–Poisson system, see
[
14
]
.

1.4.
Main difficulties and proof strategy

Due to the presence of the self-consistent electric field, the main difficulty in constructing a unique stationary solution to the VPB system lies in obtaining suitable stationary regularity estimates. The existence and uniqueness arguments rely on these estimates together with a sequential construction scheme.
The dynamical problem is closely related and follows similar ideas; for this reason, we mainly focus on the stationary case in the discussion of the key difficulties. The corresponding differences and additional ideas in the dynamical setting are described in Remark
5

•

A priori
W^{1,p}
estimate

In the stationary problem, a major difficulty in deriving stationary regularity estimates lies in controlling the integral operator
K
and its associated kernel
\mathbf{k}
(see Section
2.2
).
For the stationary Boltzmann equation, the key idea in
[
5
]
is to exploit the mixing effect along characteristics to gain regularity.
Specifically, a double Duhamel formula is employed, leading to the expression

\displaystyle\int^{t}_{0}\mathrm{d}s\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}u\mathbf{k}(v,u)\int^{s}_{0}\mathrm{d}s^{\prime}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}\mathbf{k}(u,u^{\prime})\nabla_{x}h(x-(t-s)v-(s-s^{\prime})u,u^{\prime}).

The additional iteration introduces the key term
(s-s^{\prime})u
in the spatial variable. Through the change of variables

\displaystyle\nabla_{x}h(x-(t-s)v-(s-s^{\prime})u,u^{\prime})=-(s-s^{\prime})^{-1}\nabla_{u}[h(x-(t-s)v-(s-s^{\prime})u,u^{\prime})],

the derivative can be removed through integration by parts in
\mathrm{d}u
when
s-s^{\prime}
is away from
0
.

In our stationary VPB problem, the characteristic is determined by the potential field, and the Duhamel formula becomes

(1.22)

\int^{t}_{0}\mathrm{d}s\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}u\mathbf{k}(V(s),u)\int^{s}_{0}\mathrm{d}s^{\prime}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}\mathbf{k}(V(s^{\prime};s,X(s),u),u^{\prime})\nabla_{x}[h(X(s^{\prime};s,X(s),u),u^{\prime})].

Applying the chain rule in this case yields

\nabla_{x}[h(X(s^{\prime};s,X(s),u),u^{\prime})]\sim\nabla_{x}X(s^{\prime};s,X(s),u)\nabla_{u}[h(X(s^{\prime};s,X(s),u),u^{\prime})]\nabla_{u}^{-1}X(s^{\prime};s,X(s),u).

However, integration by parts in
\mathrm{d}u
requires control of the second derivative
\nabla_{u}[\nabla_{x}X(s^{\prime};s,X(s),u)]
, which depends on the third-order derivative
\|\nabla_{x}^{3}\phi_{h}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}
.
This in turn leads to the need for control of second-order derivatives of
h
, which are not expected even for the pure Boltzmann equation
[
26
]
.

To overcome this difficulty, we observe that the third-order derivative control of
\nabla_{x}^{3}\phi_{f}
can be expected in
L^{p}
spaces. As demonstrated in
[
6
]
, in a convex domain, the optimal Sobolev regularity for the Boltzmann equation is
W^{1,p}
with
p<3
(see also
[
10
]
). By the Calderón–Zygmund estimates,

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{x}^{3}\phi_{h}\|_{L^{p}_{x}}\lesssim\|h\|_{L^{p}_{x,v}}+\|\nabla_{x}h\|_{L^{p}_{x,v}}.

This provides control of
\|\nabla_{x,v}X(s;t,x,v)\|_{L^{p}_{x,v}}
, as shown in the Lemma
7
.

We note that the
W^{1,p}
estimate in
[
6
]
is derived from a weighted point-wise
C^{1}
estimate. In our setting, the
W^{1,p}
regularity must be constructed independently. Due to the absence of a Gronwall inequality in the stationary problem, the
W^{1,p}
estimate is constructed directly through the method of characteristics. In this framework, the problematic term (
1.22
) can be controlled under the
L^{p}
norm after integration by parts.
The integrability in
v
within
L^{p}_{v}
arises from carefully applying the Hölder inequality to extract
\langle v\rangle^{-1}
from the damping factor
\nu(v)
in time integration and the integration against
\mathbf{k}(v,u)\mathrm{d}u
. This imposes the condition
p>2
, which holds in convex domains.
Another difficulty in the
W^{1,p}
estimate arises from the singularity of the derivative of the backward exit time
t_{\mathbf{b}}
(see (
2.2
)), given by

\nabla_{x}t_{\mathbf{b}}\sim\frac{1}{n(x_{\mathbf{b}})\cdot v_{\mathbf{b}}}.

To control the integral of the singularity in
L^{p}_{x,v}
space, we crucially apply the change of variable in Lemma
15
, which reduces the singular exponent to
p-1
.
Next, we exploit the external potential field to control the forward exit time
t_{\mathbf{f}}
(see (
2.2
)) through the estimate in (
2.33
),

t_{\mathbf{f}}\lesssim|n(x)\cdot v|,

which further reduces the exponent to
p-2
.
Such an estimate generally fails without the external field, particularly in the small-velocity regime. We refer to Section
4
for further details.

•

W^{1,p}
–
\alpha C^{1}_{x,v}
bootstrap and
C^{1}_{v}
estimate without kinetic weight

To control the second derivative
\|\nabla_{x}^{2}\phi_{h}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}
in the three-dimensional domain, weighted
C^{1}
regularity remains necessary, as shown in Lemma
18
and Lemma
19
. To circumvent the aforementioned difficulty with
\|\nabla^{3}_{x}\phi_{h}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}
, our key observation is that the
W^{1,p}
estimate allows us to implement a
W^{1,p}
–
\alpha C^{1}
bootstrap argument, converting point-wise regularity into
W^{1,p}
control.
This argument avoids integration by parts and therefore does not require second derivatives of
X(s)
. We note that this bootstrap argument shares the spirit of the
L^{2}
–
L^{\infty}
argument in
[
23
]
, which applied to the double Duhamel formula without taking derivatives.

As a key component of this bootstrap argument, when the external potential field
\phi_{E}
dominates near the boundary, distance information can be incorporated into the kinetic weight
\alpha_{h}
(see Remark
3
). Such a weight was introduced in
[
2
]
for local-in-time solutions, remaining constant at a distance
\delta
from the boundary while compensating for the singularity
n(x_{\mathbf{b}})\cdot v_{\mathbf{b}}
near the grazing set
\gamma_{0}
.
To adapt this weight to the stationary and global setting, we observe that, due to the presence of
\phi_{E}
, the characteristic can stay near the boundary only for a finite time, as shown in (
2.35
) in Lemma
5
. This observation yields control of the variation of
\phi_{E}
along the characteristic in Lemma
13
. Consequently, the nonlocal-to-local estimate in Lemma
17
remains valid for large-time integration.

Furthermore, justifying dynamical stability requires a
C^{1}_{v}
estimate without kinetic weight (see Remark
5
). This is not generally true without an external field
\nabla_{x}\phi_{E}
. In our setting, the external field provides control of
t_{\mathbf{b}}
via the singularity
|n(x)\cdot v|
, as shown in (
2.33
). This estimate is crucial in controlling the
v
-derivative of the characteristic in Lemma
3
, where the denominator singularity is compensated by
t_{\mathbf{b}}
. We refer to Section
5
for further details.

•

Uniqueness argument.

In
[
3
]
, uniqueness is proved in the space
L^{3}_{x}L^{1+}_{v}
by applying Gronwall’s inequality in the dynamical setting. However, in the stationary problem, this approach is no longer available, as Gronwall’s inequality cannot be used.
Since the linear collision operator
\mathcal{L}
(see Section
2.2
) is coercive only on
L^{2}_{v}
, we work in
L^{2}_{v}
and control
\|h_{1}-h_{2}\|_{L^{2}_{x,v}}
.

The microscopic component
\|(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{P})(h_{1}-h_{2})\|_{L^{2}_{x,v}}
is controlled by the coercivity of
\mathcal{L}
. For the macroscopic component
\|\mathbf{P}(h_{1}-h_{2})\|_{L^{2}_{x,v}}
, we employ the dual argument in
[
15
,
16
]
.
In the equation for
h_{1}-h_{2}
, the main problematic term is

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}|\nabla_{x}\phi_{h_{1}-h_{2}}\cdot\nabla_{v}h_{2}||h_{1}-h_{2}|\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}v.

In the
L^{2}_{x,v}
framework, this term requires at least
L^{2}_{v}
control of
\nabla_{v}h_{2}
, which is not available in
[
3
]
. However, in our setting with external force, the
v
-derivative enjoys a
C^{1}_{v}
estimate without kinetic weight, as mentioned above. Consequently, the problematic term can be controlled by

\|h_{1}-h_{2}\|_{L^{2}_{x,v}}\|\nabla_{x}\phi_{h_{1}-h_{2}}\|_{L^{2}_{x}}\|\nabla_{v}h_{2}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x,v}}\lesssim\|h_{1}-h_{2}\|_{L^{2}_{x,v}}^{2}\|\nabla_{v}h_{2}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x,v}}.

We refer to Section
6
for further details.

•

Existence argument.

To establish the existence of a stationary solution, we begin with the following sequential construction (see (
7.1
)–(
7.4
) for details): for any
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
,

(1.23)

\begin{split}v\cdot\nabla_{x}h^{\ell+1}&-\nabla_{x}(\phi^{\ell}_{h}+\phi_{E})\cdot\nabla_{v}h^{\ell+1}+\frac{v\cdot\nabla_{x}\phi^{\ell}_{h}}{2}h^{\ell+1}+e^{-\phi_{E}}\mathcal{L}h^{\ell+1}\\
&=-(v\cdot\nabla_{x}\phi^{\ell+1}_{h})e^{-\phi_{E}/2}\sqrt{\mu}+e^{-\phi_{E}/2}\Gamma(h^{\ell},h^{\ell}),\\
-\Delta\phi^{i}_{h}&=e^{-\phi_{E}/2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}h^{i}\sqrt{\mu}\mathrm{d}v\text{ in }\Omega,\ i\in\{\ell,\ell+1\}.\end{split}

We emphasize that the term
\nabla_{x}\phi^{\ell+1}_{h}
appears on the right-hand side. This choice is crucial, as it enables us to obtain the key estimate
\|\mathbf{P}h^{\ell+1}\|_{L^{2}_{x,\nu}}^{2}
in (
7.6
), which in turn yields uniform-in-
\ell
bounds for
h^{\ell+1}
(see Lemma
24
).
We then show that
\{h^{\ell}\}_{\ell=0}^{\infty}
forms a Cauchy sequence in
L^{2}_{x,\nu}(\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3})\cap L^{2}(\gamma_{+})
, and its limit is a weak solution to the steady problem.

The main difficulty lies in establishing the well-posedness of the iterative scheme (
1.23
). Indeed, classical well-posedness results typically apply only when the right-hand side depends solely on the
\ell
-th iterate, whereas here it also involves the
(\ell+1)
-th order term through
\phi_{h}^{\ell+1}
.
To overcome this difficulty, we introduce a sufficiently small parameter
0<\lambda\ll 1
, a pair of given external functions
(g,\phi_{g})
, and a sequence of source terms
\{S_{k}\}^{\infty}_{k=1}
.
We then consider the following auxiliary system:

(1.24)

\begin{split}v\cdot\nabla_{x}h&-\nabla_{x}(\phi_{g}+\phi_{E})\cdot\nabla_{v}h+\frac{v\cdot\nabla_{x}\phi_{g}}{2}h+e^{-\phi_{E}}\mathcal{L}h\\
&=-k\lambda(v\cdot\nabla_{x}\phi_{h})e^{-\phi_{E}/2}\sqrt{\mu}+e^{-\phi_{E}/2}\Gamma(g,g)+S_{k},\\
-\Delta\phi_{h}&=e^{-\phi_{E}/2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}h\sqrt{\mu}\mathrm{d}v\text{ in }\Omega.\end{split}

We begin by establishing the well-posedness of (
1.24
) (see Proposition
12
) in the case
k=1
.
Thanks to the smallness of
\lambda
, the well-posedness of this system follows from a sequential argument combined with a priori estimates.
Next, in Proposition
15
, we extend the result from
\lambda
to
N\lambda=1
(i.e.,
k=N
).
The key idea is to rewrite the right-hand side of the first equation in (
1.24
) as

\begin{split}&-k\lambda(v\cdot\nabla_{x}\phi_{h})e^{-\phi_{E}/2}\sqrt{\mu}+e^{-\phi_{E}/2}\Gamma(g,g)+S_{k}\\
&=-(k-1)\lambda(v\cdot\nabla_{x}\phi_{h})e^{-\phi_{E}/2}\sqrt{\mu}+e^{-\phi_{E}/2}\Gamma(g,g)+\underbrace{\big(S_{k}-\lambda(v\cdot\nabla_{x}\phi_{h})e^{-\phi_{E}/2}\sqrt{\mu}\big)}_{\tilde{S_{k}}}.\end{split}

This allows us to treat the system inductively. Specifically, assuming that for some
C>0
,

(1.25)

\begin{split}\|wS_{k}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x,v}}+\|S_{k}\|_{L^{2}_{x,v}}\lesssim C[|f_{b}|_{L^{2}_{\gamma_{-}}}+|wf_{b}|_{L^{\infty}_{\partial\Omega,v}}].\end{split}

By a priori estimates and induction, we obtain that

(1.26)

\begin{split}\|wh\|_{L^{\infty}_{x,v}}+|h|_{L^{2}_{\gamma_{+}}}+\|h\|_{L^{2}_{x,\nu}}&\lesssim(C+1)[|f_{b}|_{L^{2}_{\gamma_{-}}}+|wf_{b}|_{L^{\infty}_{\partial\Omega,v}}].\end{split}

We note that the factor
C+1
in (
1.26
) arises from the additional term
\lambda(v\cdot\nabla_{x}\phi_{h})e^{-\phi_{E}/2}\sqrt{\mu}
absorbed into
\tilde{S_{k}}
.
Moreover, similar regularity estimates for
\partial_{x,v}h
hold as in (
7.141
) of Proposition
15
.
Finally, we set
S_{N}=0
for
k=N
with
N\lambda=1
. In this case,
S_{N}
satisfies (
1.25
) with
C=0
, and the corresponding estimate (
1.26
) holds.
Then, by replacing the prescribed function
g
with the sequence
\{h^{\ell}\}_{\ell=0}^{\infty}
in (
1.24
), we recover the original scheme (
1.23
), thereby establishing its well-posedness (see Theorem
11
).
We refer to Section
7.3
for further details.

1.5.
Outline of the paper

The rest of the paper is organized as follows:
We collect several preliminary estimates in Section
2
, including properties of the characteristics, the collision operators, and the kinetic weight.
Sections
3
,
4
, and
5
establish a priori estimates for the stationary VPB system: the
L^{2}
–
L^{\infty}
estimate, the
W^{1,p}
estimate, and the
\alpha
-
C^{1}_{x,v}
and
C^{1}_{v}
estimates without kinetic weight, respectively.
In Section
6
, we prove the uniqueness of stationary solutions under a smallness condition on the unweighted
C^{1}_{v}
estimate.
Based on the estimates and the uniqueness argument in Sections
3
–
6
, Section
7
constructs stationary solutions via a sequential scheme. For clarity, the a priori estimates, the well-posedness, and the existence argument are presented separately.
Finally, Section
8
studies the dynamical problem around the stationary solution and establishes dynamical stability as well as uniform-in-time regularity, as stated in Theorem
2
.

1.6.
Notation

We introduce the following notational conventions.
The relation
f\lesssim g
(resp.
f\gtrsim g
) denotes that there exists a constant
C>0
such that
f\leq Cg
(resp.
f\geq Cg
). The notation
\partial_{x,v}f
denotes either a first-order spatial derivative or a first-order velocity derivative of
f
. Thus,
|\partial_{x,v}f|\lesssim 1
means
|\partial_{x}f|+|\partial_{v}f|\lesssim 1
, and
|\partial_{x,v}f|\lesssim|\partial_{x,v}g|
means
|\partial_{x}f|+|\partial_{v}f|\lesssim|\partial_{x}g|+|\partial_{v}g|
. The operator
\mathbf{I}
denotes the identity operator.
Throughout the paper, we introduce the velocity weights

w(v)=e^{\theta|v|^{2}},\qquad w_{\tilde{\theta}}(v)=e^{\tilde{\theta}|v|^{2}},\ \text{ with }0<\tilde{\theta}\ll\theta<\frac{1}{4}.

Define the boundary layer of the domain for
d>0
by

\Omega_{d}:=\{x\in\Omega:\mathrm{dist}(x,\partial\Omega)<d\}.

The boundary measure is given by

\mathrm{d}\gamma=|n(x)\cdot v|\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}S_{x},

where
dS_{x}
denotes the surface measure on
\partial\Omega
.
For
1\leq p<\infty
, we define the boundary norms

|f|_{L^{p}_{\gamma_{\pm}}}^{p}:=\int_{\gamma_{\pm}}|f(x,v)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}\gamma,\qquad|f|_{L^{\infty}_{\partial\Omega,v}}:=\operatorname*{ess\,sup}_{(x,v)\in\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}|f(x,v)|.

We also introduce the following norms:

\displaystyle\|f\|_{L^{2}_{\nu}}:=\|\nu^{1/2}f\|_{L^{2}_{v}},\qquad\|f\|_{L^{2}_{x,\nu}}:=\|\nu^{1/2}f\|_{L^{2}_{x,v}},

\displaystyle\|f\|_{L^{2}_{T,x}}:=\Big(\int_{0}^{T}\|f(t)\|_{L^{2}_{x}}^{2}\mathrm{d}t\Big)^{1/2},\quad\|f\|_{L^{2}_{T,x,v}}:=\Big(\int_{0}^{T}\|f(t)\|_{L^{2}_{x,v}}^{2}\mathrm{d}t\Big)^{1/2},

\displaystyle\|f\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}L^{p}_{x,v}}:=\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}\|f(s)\|_{L^{p}_{x,v}},\quad|f|_{L^{2}_{T,\gamma_{\pm}}}:=\Big(\int_{0}^{T}|f|_{L^{2}_{\gamma_{\pm}}}^{2}\mathrm{d}t\Big)^{1/2}.

2.
Preliminary

In this section, we collect several preliminary estimates. All estimates hold for both the stationary problem (
1.7
) and the dynamical problem (
1.17
).
For simplicity, we use
f
and
\phi_{f}
to represent both settings throughout this section (except in Lemmas
8
,
9
,
18
, and
19
, where the stationary and dynamical settings are treated separately)
1
1

1

In later sections, we will specify whether we are considering
h(x,v)
in (
1.7
) or
f(t,x,v)
in (
1.17
).
.
The characteristics of (
1.7
) and (
1.17
) are determined by the Hamiltonian ODEs

(2.1)

\begin{cases}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}X_{f}(s;t,x,v)=V_{f}(s;t,x,v),\\[5.0pt]
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}V_{f}(s;t,x,v)=-\nabla_{x}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\big(s,X_{f}(s;t,x,v)\big),\end{cases}

for
-\infty<s,t<\infty
, with
(X_{f}(t;t,x,v),V_{f}(t;t,x,v))=(x,v)
.
For simplicity, we omit the subscript
f
and write

X(s;t,x,v):=X_{f}(s;t,x,v),\qquad V(s;t,x,v):=V_{f}(s;t,x,v).

We now define the backward and forward exit times by

(2.2)

\begin{split}t_{\mathbf{b}}(t,x,v)&:=\sup\left\{s\geq 0:X(t-\tau;t,x,v)\in\Omega\ \text{for all }\tau\in[0,s)\right\},\\
t_{\mathbf{f}}(t,x,v)&:=\sup\left\{s\geq 0:X(t+\tau;t,x,v)\in\Omega\ \text{for all }\tau\in[0,s)\right\}.\end{split}

Moreover, we define the backward exit position and velocity by

x_{\mathbf{b}}(t,x,v):=X(t-t_{\mathbf{b}};t,x,v),\qquad v_{\mathbf{b}}(t,x,v):=V(t-t_{\mathbf{b}};t,x,v).

Recall from Section
1.1
that
\{\mathcal{O}_{p}\}_{p\in\mathcal{P}}
, with
\mathcal{P}=\tilde{\mathcal{P}}\cup\{0\}
, forms an open covering of
\bar{\Omega}
.
For every backward exit position
x_{\mathbf{b}}(x,v)\in\partial\Omega
, we choose
p^{1}\in\mathcal{P}
satisfying
x_{\mathbf{b}}(x,v)\in\mathcal{O}_{p^{1}}
, and denote

\mathbf{x}_{p^{1}}^{1}:=(\mathbf{x}^{1}_{p^{1},1},\mathbf{x}^{1}_{p^{1},2},0)\ \text{ such that }\ \eta_{p^{1}}(\mathbf{x}_{p^{1}}^{1})=x_{\mathbf{b}}(x,v).

In addition, for the stationary problem (
1.7
), the corresponding backward and forward exit times, positions, and velocities are all time-independent.

2.1.
Estimate for the characteristic

Lemma 2
.

For some fixed
t>0
, assume that
\|\nabla_{x}^{2}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}t+\|\nabla_{x}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\ll 1
.

(1)

For any
\max\{0,t-t_{\mathbf{b}}\}\leq s\leq t
,

(2.3)

\displaystyle|\nabla_{v}X(s;t,x,v)|\lesssim|t-s|e^{\|\nabla_{x}^{2}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\frac{(t-s)^{2}}{2}}\lesssim|t-s|e^{o(1)(t-s)},

(2.4)

\displaystyle|\nabla_{v}V(s;t,x,v)|\lesssim 1+|t-s|e^{\|\nabla_{x}^{2}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\frac{(t-s)^{2}}{2}}\lesssim(1+|t-s|)e^{o(1)(t-s)}.

Further, suppose that
\|\nabla_{x}^{2}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}(t)^{2}e^{t}\ll 1
. Then we have

(2.5)

|\det(\nabla_{v}X(s;t,x,v))|\gtrsim|t-s|^{3},\qquad|(\partial_{v}X(s;t,x,v))^{-1}|\lesssim|t-s|^{-1}.

(2)

For any
\max\{0,t-t_{\mathbf{b}}\}\leq s\leq t
,

(2.6)

\displaystyle|\nabla_{x}X(s;t,x,v)|\lesssim e^{\|\nabla_{x}^{2}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\frac{(t-s)^{2}}{2}}\lesssim e^{o(1)(t-s)},

(2.7)

\displaystyle|\nabla_{x}V(s;t,x,v)|\lesssim e^{\|\nabla_{x}^{2}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\frac{(t-s)^{2}}{2}}\lesssim e^{o(1)(t-s)}.

Moreover, suppose that
\|\nabla_{x}^{2}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}(t)^{2}e^{t}\ll 1
. Then we have

(2.8)

\big|\partial_{x}X(s;t,x,v)\big|\gtrsim 1.

Proof.

First, we prove part
(a)
.
From (
2.1
), we have

\begin{split}\Big|\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\nabla_{v}X(s;t,x,v)\Big|&\lesssim|\nabla_{v}V(s;t,x,v)|,\\
\Big|\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\nabla_{v}V(s;t,x,v)\Big|&\lesssim\|\nabla_{x}^{2}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}|\nabla_{v}X(s;t,x,v)|.\end{split}

Since
\nabla_{v}X(t;t,x,v)=\nabla_{v}x=0
,
\nabla_{v}V(t;t,x,v)=\nabla_{v}v=Id_{3\times 3}
. Then we obtain

(2.9)

\begin{split}|\nabla_{v}X(s;t,x,v)|&\lesssim\int^{t}_{s}|\nabla_{v}V(s_{1};t,x,v)|\mathrm{d}s_{1}\\
&\lesssim|t-s|+\int^{t}_{s}\int^{t}_{s_{1}}\|\nabla_{x}^{2}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}|\nabla_{v}X(s_{2};t,x,v)|\mathrm{d}s_{2}\mathrm{d}s_{1}.\end{split}

Using Fubini’s theorem, we derive

(2.10)

\begin{split}\eqref{eq1:Xv first}&\lesssim|t-s|+\int^{t}_{s}\int^{s_{2}}_{s}\|\nabla_{x}^{2}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}|\nabla_{v}X(s_{2};t,x,v)|\mathrm{d}s_{1}\mathrm{d}s_{2}\\
&\lesssim|t-s|+\int^{t}_{s}(s_{2}-s)\|\nabla_{x}^{2}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}|\nabla_{v}X(s_{2};t,x,v)|\mathrm{d}s_{2}.\end{split}

Since
\max\{0,t-t_{\mathbf{b}}\}\leq s\leq t
, the Gronwall’s inequality implies that

\displaystyle|\nabla_{v}X(s;t,x,v)|\lesssim|t-s|\exp\big(\|\nabla_{x}^{2}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\int^{t}_{s}(s_{2}-s)\mathrm{d}s_{2}\big)\leq|t-s|e^{\|\nabla_{x}^{2}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\frac{(t-s)^{2}}{2}}.

Similar to (
2.9
), we also obtain

\begin{split}|\nabla_{v}V(s;t,x,v)|&\lesssim 1+\int^{t}_{s}\|\nabla_{x}^{2}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}|\nabla_{v}X(s_{1};t,x,v)|\mathrm{d}s_{1}<\infty.\end{split}

This, together with (
2.10
) and the Gronwall’s inequality, implies that

\begin{split}|\nabla_{v}X(s;t,x,v)|+|\nabla_{v}V(s;t,x,v)|&\lesssim 1+|t-s|+\int^{t}_{s}(s_{2}-s+1)\|\nabla_{x}^{2}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}|\nabla_{v}X(s_{2};t,x,v)|\mathrm{d}s_{2}\\
&\lesssim|t-s+1|e^{\|\nabla_{x}^{2}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\big(\frac{1}{2}(t-s)^{2}+(t-s)\big)}.\end{split}

and thus we conclude (
2.3
) and (
2.4
).

We now prove (
2.5
).
The characteristics (
2.1
) implies that

(2.11)

\begin{split}\nabla_{v}X(s;t,x,v)&=\nabla_{v}\big(x-\int^{t}_{s}\big(v-\int^{t}_{s_{1}}-\nabla_{x}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})(X(s_{2};t,x,v))\mathrm{d}s_{2}\big)\mathrm{d}s_{1}\big)\\
&=-\int^{t}_{s}\big(Id_{3\times 3}+\int^{t}_{s_{1}}\nabla_{x}^{2}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})(X(s_{2};t,x,v))\nabla_{v}X(s_{2};t,x,v)\mathrm{d}s_{2}\big)\mathrm{d}s_{1}\\
&=-(t-s)Id_{3\times 3}-\underbrace{\int^{t}_{s}\int^{t}_{s_{1}}\nabla_{x}^{2}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})(X(s_{2};t,x,v))\nabla_{v}X(s_{2};t,x,v)\mathrm{d}s_{2}\mathrm{d}s_{1}}_{\eqref{eq1:Xv second}^{*}}.\end{split}

Using (
2.3
) and
0\leq s\leq t
, we have

(2.12)

\begin{split}|\eqref{eq1:Xv second}^{*}|&\leq\int^{t}_{s}\int^{t}_{s_{1}}|\nabla_{x}^{2}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})(X(s_{2};t,x,v))||\nabla_{v}X(s_{2};t,x,v)|\mathrm{d}s_{2}\mathrm{d}s_{1}\\
&\leq\|\nabla_{x}^{2}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\int^{t}_{s}\int^{t}_{s_{1}}|t-s_{2}|e^{\|\nabla_{x}^{2}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\frac{(t-s_{2})^{2}}{2}}\mathrm{d}s_{2}\mathrm{d}s_{1}\\
&\lesssim(t-s)e^{\|\nabla_{x}^{2}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\frac{(t)^{2}}{2}}\|\nabla_{x}^{2}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}(t)^{2}.\end{split}

Further, assume that
\|\nabla_{x}^{2}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}(t)^{2}e^{t}\ll 1
.
From (
2.11
) and (
2.12
), we derive that

\nabla_{v}X(s;t,x,v)=-(t-s)\Big(Id_{3\times 3}+o(1)M\Big),

where
M\in\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3}
is a matrix satisfying
|M|\lesssim 1
. This concludes (
2.5
).

Second, we prove part
(b)
.
From (
2.1
), we have

\begin{split}\Big|\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\nabla_{x}X(s;t,x,v)\Big|&\lesssim|\nabla_{x}V(s;t,x,v)|,\\
\Big|\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\nabla_{x}V(s;t,x,v)\Big|&\lesssim\|\nabla_{x}^{2}\phi_{f}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}|\nabla_{x}X(s;t,x,v)|.\end{split}

Since
\nabla_{x}X(t;t,x,v)=\nabla_{x}x=1
,
\nabla_{x}V(t;t,x,v)=\nabla_{x}v=0
. Then we obtain

(2.13)

\begin{split}|\nabla_{x}X(s;t,x,v)|&\lesssim 1+\int^{t}_{s}|\nabla_{x}V(s_{1};t,x,v)|\mathrm{d}s_{1}\\
&\lesssim 1+\int^{t}_{s}\int^{t}_{s_{1}}\|\nabla_{x}^{2}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}|\nabla_{x}X(s_{2};t,x,v)|\mathrm{d}s_{2}\mathrm{d}s_{1}.\end{split}

Using Fubini’s theorem, we derive

(2.14)

\begin{split}\eqref{eq1:Xx first}&\lesssim 1+\int^{t}_{s}\int^{s_{2}}_{s}\|\nabla_{x}^{2}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}|\nabla_{x}X(s_{2};t,x,v)|\mathrm{d}s_{1}\mathrm{d}s_{2}\\
&\lesssim 1+\int^{t}_{s}(s_{2}-s)\|\nabla_{x}^{2}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}|\nabla_{x}X(s_{2};t,x,v)|\mathrm{d}s_{2}.\end{split}

Since
\max\{0,t-t_{\mathbf{b}}\}\leq s\leq t\leq T_{0}
, the Gronwall’s inequality implies that

\begin{split}|\nabla_{v}X(s;t,x,v)|&\lesssim\exp\big(\|\nabla_{x}^{2}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\int^{t}_{s}(s_{2}-s)\mathrm{d}s_{2}\big)\leq e^{\|\nabla_{x}^{2}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\frac{(t-s)^{2}}{2}},\\
|\nabla_{v}V(s;t,x,v)|&\lesssim\int^{t}_{s}\|\nabla_{x}^{2}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}|\nabla_{x}X(s_{1};t,x,v)|\mathrm{d}s_{1}<\infty.\end{split}

This, together with (
2.14
) and the Gronwall’s inequality, implies that

\begin{split}|\nabla_{v}X(s;t,x,v)|+|\nabla_{v}V(s;t,x,v)|&\lesssim 1+\int^{t}_{s}(s_{2}-s+1)\|\nabla_{x}^{2}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}|\nabla_{v}X(s_{2};t,x,v)|\mathrm{d}s_{2}\\
&\lesssim e^{\|\nabla_{x}^{2}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\big(\frac{1}{2}(t-s)^{2}+(t-s)\big)}.\end{split}

and thus we conclude (
2.6
) and (
2.7
).

We now prove (
2.8
).
The characteristics (
2.1
) implies that

(2.15)

\begin{split}\nabla_{x}X(s;t,x,v)&=\nabla_{x}\big(x-\int^{t}_{s}\big(v-\int^{t}_{s_{1}}-\nabla_{x}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})(X(s_{2};t,x,v))\mathrm{d}s_{2}\big)\mathrm{d}s_{1}\big)\\
&=1-\int^{t}_{s}\int^{t}_{s_{1}}\nabla_{x}^{2}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})(X(s_{2};t,x,v))\nabla_{x}X(s_{2};t,x,v)\mathrm{d}s_{2}\mathrm{d}s_{1}\\
&=1-\underbrace{\int^{t}_{s}\int^{t}_{s_{1}}\nabla_{x}^{2}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})(X(s_{2};t,x,v))\nabla_{x}X(s_{2};t,x,v)\mathrm{d}s_{2}\mathrm{d}s_{1}}_{\eqref{eq1:Xx second}^{*}}.\end{split}

Using (
2.6
) and
0\leq s\leq t
, we have

(2.16)

\begin{split}|\eqref{eq1:Xx second}^{*}|&\leq\int^{t}_{s}\int^{t}_{s_{1}}|\nabla_{x}^{2}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})(X(s_{2};t,x,v))||\nabla_{x}X(s_{2};t,x,v)|\mathrm{d}s_{2}\mathrm{d}s_{1}\\
&\lesssim e^{\|\nabla_{x}^{2}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\frac{(t)^{2}}{2}}\|\nabla_{x}^{2}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}(t)^{2}.\end{split}

Further, assume that
\|\nabla_{x}^{2}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}(t)^{2}e^{t}\ll 1
.
From (
2.15
) and (
2.16
), we derive that

\big|\nabla_{x}X(s;t,x,v)\big|\gtrsim 1-e^{\|\nabla_{x}^{2}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\frac{(t)^{2}}{2}}\|\nabla_{x}^{2}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}(t)^{2}\gtrsim 1.

∎

Lemma 3
.

Fix some
t>0
. Assume that
t_{\mathbf{b}}(t,x,v)<t
. Then we have

(2.17)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial t_{\mathbf{b}}(t,x,v)}{\partial v_{j}}=

\displaystyle-\frac{t_{\mathbf{b}}(t,x,v)}{v_{\mathbf{b}}\cdot n(x_{\mathbf{b}})}\big[e_{j}+O(\|\phi_{f}+\phi_{E}\|_{C^{2}})|t_{\mathbf{b}}|^{2}e^{\|\nabla_{x}^{2}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\|_{L^{\infty}}t_{\mathbf{b}}^{2}}\big]\cdot n(x_{\mathbf{b}}),

(2.18)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial t_{\mathbf{b}}(t,x,v)}{\partial x_{j}}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{v_{\mathbf{b}}\cdot n(x_{\mathbf{b}})}\big[e_{j}+O(\|\phi_{f}+\phi_{E}\|_{C^{2}})|t_{\mathbf{b}}|^{2}e^{\|\nabla_{x}^{2}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}t_{\mathbf{b}}^{2}}\big]\cdot n(x_{\mathbf{b}}),

\displaystyle\frac{\partial v_{\mathbf{b}}(t,x,v)}{\partial v_{j}}=

\displaystyle e_{j}-\frac{t_{\mathbf{b}}(t,x,v)}{v_{\mathbf{b}}\cdot n(x_{\mathbf{b}})}\big[e_{j}+O(\|\phi_{f}+\phi_{E}\|_{C^{2}})|t_{\mathbf{b}}|^{2}e^{\|\nabla_{x}^{2}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}t_{\mathbf{b}}^{2}}\big]\cdot n(x_{\mathbf{b}})\|\nabla_{x}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}

(2.19)

\displaystyle-|t_{\mathbf{b}}|^{2}\|\nabla_{x}^{2}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}e^{\|\nabla_{x}^{2}\phi_{f}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\frac{|t-\tau|^{2}}{2}},

\displaystyle\frac{\partial v_{\mathbf{b}}(t,x,v)}{\partial x_{j}}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{v_{\mathbf{b}}\cdot n(x_{\mathbf{b}})}\big[e_{j}+O(\|\phi_{f}+\phi_{E}\|_{C^{2}})|t_{\mathbf{b}}|^{2}e^{\|\nabla_{x}^{2}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}t_{\mathbf{b}}^{2}}\big]\cdot n(x_{\mathbf{b}})\|\nabla_{x}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}

(2.20)

\displaystyle-|t_{\mathbf{b}}|\|\nabla_{x}^{2}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}e^{\|\nabla_{x}^{2}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\frac{|t-\tau|^{2}}{2}},

\displaystyle\frac{\partial x_{\mathbf{b}}(t,x,v)}{\partial v_{j}}=

\displaystyle e_{j}t_{\mathbf{b}}-\frac{t_{\mathbf{b}}(t,x,v)}{v_{\mathbf{b}}\cdot n(x_{\mathbf{b}})}\big[e_{j}+O(\|\phi_{f}+\phi_{E}\|_{C^{2}})|t_{\mathbf{b}}|^{2}e^{\|\nabla_{x}^{2}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}t_{\mathbf{b}}^{2}}\big]\cdot n(x_{\mathbf{b}})v_{\mathbf{b}}

(2.21)

\displaystyle+|t_{\mathbf{b}}|^{3}\|\nabla_{x}^{2}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}e^{\|\nabla_{x}^{2}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\frac{|t-\tau|^{2}}{2}},

\displaystyle\frac{\partial x_{\mathbf{b}}(t,x,v)}{\partial x_{j}}=

\displaystyle e_{j}+\frac{1}{v_{\mathbf{b}}\cdot n(x_{\mathbf{b}})}\big[e_{j}+O(\|\phi_{f}+\phi_{E}\|_{C^{2}})|t_{\mathbf{b}}|^{2}e^{\|\nabla_{x}^{2}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}t_{\mathbf{b}}^{2}}\big]\cdot n(x_{\mathbf{b}})v_{\mathbf{b}}

(2.22)

\displaystyle-|t_{\mathbf{b}}|^{2}\|\nabla_{x}^{2}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}e^{\|\nabla_{x}^{2}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\frac{|t-\tau|^{2}}{2}}.

For
i=1,2
,
j=1,2,3
,

(2.23)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\mathbf{x}_{p^{1},i}^{1}}{\partial x_{j}}=\frac{\partial x_{\mathbf{b}}(t,x,v)}{\partial x_{j}}\cdot\frac{\partial_{i}\eta_{p^{1}}(\mathbf{x}_{p^{1}}^{1})}{|\partial_{i}\eta_{p^{1}}(\mathbf{x}_{p^{1}}^{1})|^{2}},

(2.24)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\mathbf{x}^{1}_{p^{1},i}}{\partial v_{j}}=\frac{\partial x_{\mathbf{b}}(t,x,v)}{v_{j}}\cdot\frac{\partial_{i}\eta_{p^{1}}(\mathbf{x}_{p^{1}}^{1})}{|\partial_{i}\eta_{p^{1}}(\mathbf{x}_{p^{1}}^{1})|^{2}}.

Proof.

Denote that
t^{1}=t-t_{\mathbf{b}}(t,x,v)
.
From the assumption
t_{\mathbf{b}}(t,x,v)<t
, then

\nabla_{x,v}t_{\mathbf{b}}
,
\nabla_{x,v}v_{\mathbf{b}}
, and
\nabla_{x,v}x_{\mathbf{b}}
are well-defined.
Following the characteristics (
2.1
), we have

(2.25)

\begin{split}\lim\limits_{s\to t^{1}}V(s;t,x,v)&=v+\int^{t}_{t^{1}}\nabla_{x}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})(X(s;t,x,v))\mathrm{d}s,\\
X(\tau;t,x,v)&=x+v(\tau-t)-\int^{t}_{\tau}\int^{t}_{s}\nabla_{x}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})(X(s_{1};t,x,v))\mathrm{d}s_{1}\mathrm{d}s.\end{split}

This implies that

\begin{split}\lim\limits_{s\to t^{1}}\frac{\partial V(s;t,x,v)}{\partial v}&=1+\frac{\partial t_{\mathbf{b}}(x,v)}{\partial v}\nabla_{x}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})(X(t^{1};t,x,v))+\int^{t}_{t^{1}}\nabla^{2}_{x}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\frac{\partial X(s;t,x,v)}{\partial v}\mathrm{d}s,\\
\sup\limits_{\tau\leq s^{\prime}\leq t}\Big|\frac{\partial X(s^{\prime};t,x,v)}{\partial v}\Big|&\leq|\tau-t|+\int^{t}_{\tau}|t-s|\|\nabla_{x}^{2}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\sup\limits_{\tau\leq s^{\prime}\leq t}\Big|\frac{\partial X(s^{\prime};t,x,v)}{\partial v}\Big|\mathrm{d}s.\end{split}

Using Gronwall’s inequality, we derive that

(2.26)

\sup\limits_{\tau\leq s^{\prime}\leq t}\Big|\frac{\partial X(s^{\prime};t,x,v)}{\partial v}\Big|\leq|\tau-t|e^{\|\nabla_{x}^{2}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\frac{|t-\tau|^{2}}{2}}.

Similarly, (
2.25
) also implies that

\begin{split}\lim\limits_{s\to t^{1}}\frac{\partial V(s;t,x,v)}{\partial x}&=\frac{\partial t_{\mathbf{b}}(t,x,v)}{\partial x}\nabla_{x}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})(X(t^{1};t,x,v))+\int^{t}_{t^{1}}\nabla_{x}^{2}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\frac{\partial X(s;t,x,v)}{\partial x}\mathrm{d}s,\\
\sup\limits_{\tau\leq s^{\prime}\leq t}\Big|\frac{\partial X(s^{\prime};t,x,v)}{\partial x}\Big|&\leq 1+\int^{t}_{\tau}|t-s|\|\nabla_{x}^{2}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\sup\limits_{\tau\leq s^{\prime}\leq t}\Big|\frac{\partial X(s^{\prime};t,x,v)}{\partial x}\Big|\mathrm{d}s.\end{split}

Using Gronwall’s inequality, we derive that

(2.27)

\sup\limits_{\tau\leq s^{\prime}\leq t}\Big|\frac{\partial X(s^{\prime};t,x,v)}{\partial x}\Big|\leq e^{\|\nabla_{x}^{2}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\frac{|t-\tau|^{2}}{2}}.

Moreover, let
\tau=t^{1}\leq t
in the second equality in (
2.25
), we obtain

(2.28)

\begin{split}X(t^{1};t,x,v)=x+v(t^{1}-t)-\int^{t}_{t^{1}}\int^{t}_{s}\nabla_{x}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})(X(s_{1};t,x,v))\mathrm{d}s_{1}\mathrm{d}s.\end{split}

First, we show (
2.17
).
Taking
\nabla_{v_{j}}
on the above equality (
2.28
), we get

(2.29)

\begin{split}\nabla_{v_{j}}[X(t^{1};t,x,v)]=&-e_{j}t_{\mathbf{b}}-\frac{\partial t_{\mathbf{b}}}{\partial v_{j}}v-\frac{\partial t_{\mathbf{b}}}{\partial v_{j}}\int^{t}_{t^{1}}\nabla_{x}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})(X(s_{1};t,x,v))\mathrm{d}s_{1}\\
&-\int^{t}_{t^{1}}\int^{t}_{s}\nabla^{2}_{x}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\frac{\partial X(s_{1};t,x,v)}{\partial v_{j}}\mathrm{d}s_{1}\mathrm{d}s.\end{split}

Since
\nabla_{x,v}[X(t^{1};t,x,v)]\cdot n(x_{\mathbf{b}})=0
, this further implies that

\begin{split}\frac{\partial t_{\mathbf{b}}(t,x,v)}{\partial v_{j}}(v_{\mathbf{b}}\cdot n(x_{\mathbf{b}}))=n(x_{\mathbf{b}})\cdot\Big(-e_{j}t_{\mathbf{b}}-\int^{t}_{t^{1}}\int^{t}_{s}\nabla^{2}_{x}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\frac{\partial X(s_{1};t,x,v)}{\partial v_{j}}\mathrm{d}s_{1}\mathrm{d}s\Big).\end{split}

Combining this with (
2.26
), we conclude (
2.17
).

Second, we show (
2.18
).
Taking
\nabla_{x_{j}}
in the equality (
2.28
), we get

(2.30)

\begin{split}\nabla_{x_{j}}[X(t^{1};t,x,v)]=&e_{j}-\frac{\partial t_{\mathbf{b}}}{\partial x_{j}}v-\frac{\partial t_{\mathbf{b}}}{\partial x_{j}}\int^{t}_{t^{1}}\nabla_{x}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})(X(s_{1};t,x,v))\mathrm{d}s_{1}\\
&-\int^{t}_{t^{1}}\int^{t}_{s}\nabla^{2}_{x}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\frac{\partial X(s_{1};t,x,v)}{\partial x_{j}}\mathrm{d}s_{1}\mathrm{d}s.\end{split}

This further implies that

\begin{split}\frac{\partial t_{\mathbf{b}}(t,x,v)}{\partial x_{j}}(v_{\mathbf{b}}\cdot n(x_{\mathbf{b}}))=n(x_{\mathbf{b}})\cdot\Big(e_{j}-\int^{t}_{t^{1}}\int^{t}_{s}\nabla^{2}_{x}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\frac{\partial X(s_{1};t,x,v)}{\partial x_{j}}\mathrm{d}s_{1}\mathrm{d}s\Big).\end{split}

Together with (
2.27
), this establishes (
2.18
).

Third, we show (
2.19
), (
2.20
), (
2.21
) and (
2.22
).
From (
2.25
), we obtain

(2.31)

v_{\mathbf{b}}(t,x,v)=v+\int^{t}_{t^{1}}\nabla_{x}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})(X(s;t,x,v))\mathrm{d}s.

Taking
\partial_{v_{j}}
and
\partial_{x_{j}}
in the equality (
2.31
), we get

\begin{split}\frac{\partial v_{\mathbf{b}}(t,x,v)}{\partial v_{j}}&=e_{j}+\frac{\partial t_{\mathbf{b}}}{\partial v_{j}}\nabla_{x}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})(X(t^{1};t,x,v))-\int^{t}_{t^{1}}\nabla^{2}_{x}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\frac{\partial X(s;t,x,v)}{\partial v_{j}}\mathrm{d}s,\\
\frac{\partial v_{\mathbf{b}}(t,x,v)}{\partial x_{j}}&=\frac{\partial t_{\mathbf{b}}}{\partial x_{j}}\nabla_{x}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})(X(t^{1};t,x,v))-\int^{t}_{t^{1}}\nabla^{2}_{x}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\frac{\partial X(s;t,x,v)}{\partial x_{j}}\mathrm{d}s.\end{split}

Combining (
2.26
) with (
2.17
), and (
2.27
) with (
2.18
), we obtain (
2.19
) and (
2.20
).
From (
2.25
), we obtain

(2.32)

x_{\mathbf{b}}(t,x,v)=x+vt_{\mathbf{b}}-\int^{t}_{t_{1}}\int^{t}_{s}\nabla_{x}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})(X(s_{1};t,x,v))\mathrm{d}s_{1}\mathrm{d}s.

Analogously, (
2.21
) and (
2.22
) are obtained by taking
\partial_{v_{j}}
and
\partial_{x_{j}}
in the equality (
2.32
), respectively.

Finally, (
2.23
) and (
2.24
) follow from

\displaystyle\partial_{x_{j}}x_{\mathbf{b}}(t,x,v)=\partial_{x_{j}}\eta_{p^{1}}(\mathbf{x}_{p^{1}}^{1})=\partial_{x_{j}}\mathbf{x}_{p^{1},1}^{1}\partial_{1}\eta_{p^{1}}(\mathbf{x}_{p^{1}}^{1})+\partial_{x_{j}}\mathbf{x}_{p^{1},2}^{1}\partial_{2}\eta_{p^{1}}(\mathbf{x}_{p^{1}}^{1}).

Taking the dot product with
\partial_{i}\eta_{p^{1}}(\mathbf{x}_{p^{1}}^{1})
, we derive

\displaystyle\partial_{x_{j}}x_{\mathbf{b}}(t,x,v)\cdot\partial_{i}\eta_{p^{1}}(\mathbf{x}_{p^{1}}^{1})=\partial_{x_{j}}\mathbf{x}_{p^{1},i}^{1}|\partial_{i}\eta_{p^{1}}(\mathbf{x}_{p^{1}}^{1})|^{2},

and this concludes (
2.23
).
The proof of (
2.24
) follows analogously by replacing
\partial_{x_{j}}
with
\partial_{v_{j}}
.
∎

Lemma 4
.

Assume that
X(s;t,x,v)\in\Omega
. Then the change of variable

(x,v)\mapsto(X(s;t,x,v),V(s;t,x,v))

has a Jacobian determinant

\displaystyle\det\Big(\frac{\partial(X(s;t,x,v),V(s;t,x,v))}{\partial(x,v)}\Big)=1.

Therefore, we conclude the following change of variable formula:

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\int_{\Omega}\mathbf{1}_{X(s;t,x,v)\in\Omega}|f(X(s;t,x,v),V(s;t,x,v))|\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}v\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\int_{\Omega}|f(x,v)|\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}v.

Proof.

Since
\nabla_{v}(\nabla_{x}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E}))=0
, the Liouville’s formula implies that

\det\Big(\frac{\partial(X(s;t,x,v),V(s;t,x,v))}{\partial(x,v)}\Big)=1.

∎

Lemma 5
.

Suppose the external field
\nabla_{x}\phi_{E}
satisfies the assumptions in Theorem
1
.
Further, assume that
\|\nabla_{x}^{2}\phi_{f}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}+\|\nabla_{x}\phi_{f}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}<\frac{1}{20}C_{E}
.
Then there exists a constant
0<\delta\ll 1
such that the following holds.

(1)

For
(x,v)\in\gamma_{+},
if
t_{\mathbf{b}}(t,x,v)<\infty
and
X(s;t,x,v)\in\Omega_{\delta}
for all
t-t_{\mathbf{b}}(t,x,v)\leq s\leq t
, then

(2.33)

t_{\mathbf{b}}(t,x,v)\lesssim|n(x)\cdot v|.

(2)

For
(x,v)\in\gamma_{-}
, if
t_{\mathbf{f}}(t,x,v)<\infty
and
X(s;t,x,v)\in\Omega_{\delta}
for all
t\leq s\leq t+t_{\mathbf{f}}
, then

(2.34)

t_{\mathbf{f}}(t,x,v)\lesssim|n(x)\cdot v|.

Moreover, if
X(s;t,x,v)\in\Omega_{\delta}
for
t_{1}\leq s\leq t_{2}
, then

(2.35)

|t_{2}-t_{1}|\lesssim_{\delta,C_{E}}1.

Proof.

For simplicity, we write
(X(s),V(s)):=(X(s;t,x,v),V(s;t,x,v))
throughout the proof.

First, we prove (
2.33
).
From (
1.2
) and (
1.4
) in Section
1.1
, there exists a
C^{2}
function
\xi(x)
and a constant
0<\delta\ll 1
such that

(2.36)

\xi(x)=-dist(x,\partial\Omega)\ \text{ when }\ dist(x,\partial\Omega)<\delta\ \text{ and }\ n(x)=\frac{\nabla_{x}\xi(x)}{|\nabla_{x}\xi(x)|},\quad x\in\partial\Omega.

Further, (
1.3
) shows that for any
y\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
,

(2.37)

y^{\intercal}\cdot\nabla_{x}^{2}\xi(x)\cdot y\gtrsim|y|^{2}.

Consider any
(x,v)\in\gamma_{+}
. For any
t-t_{\mathbf{b}}(x,v)<s<t
, we compute that

(2.38)

\begin{split}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}(-\xi(X(s)))&=-V(s)\cdot\nabla_{x}\xi(X(s)),\\
\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}s^{2}}(-\xi(X(s)))&=\nabla_{x}\xi(X(s))\cdot\nabla_{x}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})-V(s)^{\intercal}\cdot\nabla_{x}^{2}\xi(X(s))\cdot V(s).\end{split}

Since
\frac{\nabla_{x}\xi(x)}{|\nabla_{x}\xi(x)|}=n(x)
, the assumptions on
\nabla_{x}\phi_{E}
and
\nabla_{x}\phi_{f}
and the continuity of
\nabla_{x}\xi
imply that for every
X(s)\in\Omega_{\delta}
,

\begin{split}\nabla_{x}\xi(X(s))\cdot\nabla_{x}\phi_{E}(X(s))&\leq-\frac{C_{E}}{2}|\nabla_{x}\xi(x)|,\\
\nabla_{x}\xi(X(s))\cdot\nabla_{x}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})(X(s))&\leq-\frac{C_{E}}{4}|\nabla_{x}\xi(x)|.\end{split}

This, together with (
2.37
) and (
2.38
), shows that for every
X(s)\in\Omega_{\delta}
,

(2.39)

\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}s^{2}}(-\xi(X(s)))\lesssim-C_{E}|\nabla_{x}\xi(x)|.

From (
2.36
) and (
2.38
), we obtain that
\xi(X(t))=0
and
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\xi(X(t))=-|\nabla_{x}\xi(x)|n(x)\cdot v<0
. With
\xi(X(t-t_{\mathbf{b}}))=0
, this implies that

t_{\mathbf{b}}(t,x,v)\lesssim\frac{1}{C_{E}}|n(x)\cdot v|,

and thus we conclude (
2.33
).
The proof of (
2.34
) follows by the same argument.

Second, we prove (
2.35
). We may assume that
X(t_{1}),X(t_{2})\in\partial\Omega_{\delta}
. Otherwise, (
2.39
) implies that there exist
t_{3}\leq t_{1}\leq t_{2}\leq t_{4}
such that
X(t_{3}),X(t_{4})\in\partial\Omega_{\delta}
and
X(s)\in\Omega_{\delta}
for all
t_{3}\leq s\leq t_{4}
. It then suffices to show that
|t_{4}-t_{3}|\lesssim_{\delta,C_{E}}1
, which directly implies the upper bound on
|t_{2}-t_{1}|
.
We now consider three cases: (1)
X(t_{1}),X(t_{2})\in\partial\Omega
, (2) one of
X(t_{1}),X(t_{2})
lies in
\partial\Omega
and the other lies in
\partial\Omega_{\delta}\setminus\partial\Omega
, (3)
X(t_{1}),X(t_{2})\in\partial\Omega_{\delta}\setminus\partial\Omega
.

Case 1:
X(t_{1}),X(t_{2})\in\partial\Omega
.

From (
1.4
), we have
\xi(X(t_{1}))=\xi(X(t_{2}))=0
and
-\delta\leq\xi(X(s))\leq 0
for all
t_{1}\leq s\leq t_{2}
.
Then there exists
t^{*}\in[t_{1},t_{2}]
such that
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\xi(X(t^{*}))=0
.
Combining with (
2.39
), we obtain

\begin{split}\delta\geq|\xi(X(t_{1}))-\xi(X(t_{*}))|=\Big|\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{*}}\int_{s}^{t_{*}}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}s^{2}}\xi(X(\tau))\mathrm{d}\tau\mathrm{d}s\Big|\gtrsim\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{*}}\int_{s}^{t_{*}}C_{E}\mathrm{d}\tau\mathrm{d}s\gtrsim(t_{*}-t_{1})^{2}C_{E}.\end{split}

This implies
|t^{*}-t_{1}|\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{\delta}{C_{E}}}
. Similarly, we obtain
|t_{2}-t^{*}|\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{\delta}{C_{E}}}
. Consequently, we derive that

|t_{2}-t_{1}|\leq|t^{*}-t_{1}|+|t_{2}-t^{*}|\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{\delta}{C_{E}}}.

Case 2:
X(t_{1})\in\partial\Omega
,
X(t_{2})\in\partial\Omega_{\delta}\setminus\partial\Omega
or
X(t_{1})\in\partial\Omega_{\delta}\setminus\partial\Omega
,
X(t_{2})\in\partial\Omega
.

It suffices to consider the case when
X(t_{1})\in\partial\Omega
and
X(t_{2})\in\partial\Omega_{\delta}\setminus\partial\Omega
, since the other case follows similarly.
Since
\xi(X(t_{1}))
and
\xi(X(t_{2}))=-\delta
, we compute that

(2.40)

\delta=\xi(X(t_{1}))-\xi(X(t_{2}))=\int^{t_{1}}_{t_{2}}\Big(\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\xi(X(s))-\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\xi(X(t_{2}))\Big)\mathrm{d}s+\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\xi(X(t_{2}))(t_{1}-t_{2}).

Since
X(s)\in\Omega_{\delta}
for all
t_{1}\leq s\leq t_{2}
, it follows that
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\xi(X(t_{2}))\leq 0
. This, together with (
2.40
), implies

\delta\geq\int^{t_{1}}_{t_{2}}\Big(\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\xi(X(s))-\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\xi(X(t_{2}))\Big)\mathrm{d}s=\int^{t_{1}}_{t_{2}}\int^{s}_{t_{2}}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}s^{2}}\xi(X(\tau))\mathrm{d}\tau\mathrm{d}s\gtrsim\int^{t_{1}}_{t_{2}}\int^{s}_{t_{2}}C_{E}\mathrm{d}\tau\mathrm{d}s\gtrsim(t_{2}-t_{1})^{2}C_{E}.

Thus, we conclude that
|t_{2}-t_{1}|\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{\delta}{C_{E}}}
.

Case 3:
X(t_{1}),X(t_{2})\in\partial\Omega_{\delta}\setminus\partial\Omega
.

Since
X(s)\in\Omega_{\delta}
for
t_{1}\leq s\leq t_{2}
, it follows that
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\xi(X(t_{2}))\leq 0
.
Recall from (
2.39
) that
\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}s^{2}}(-\xi(X(s)))\lesssim-C_{E}
. This implies that
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\xi(X(s))\leq 0
for all
t_{1}\leq s\leq t_{2}
. Hence, we conclude that
X(t_{1})\notin\partial\Omega_{\delta}\setminus\partial\Omega
.
Therefore, this case does not occur.
∎

The following corollary is a direct consequence of Lemma
3
and Lemma
5
.

Corollary 3
.

Assume the assumptions of Lemma
5
hold. There exists a constant
0<\delta\ll 1
such that if
X(s;t,x,v)\in\Omega_{\delta}
for all
t-t_{\mathbf{b}}(t,x,v)\leq s\leq t
, then

\begin{split}t_{\mathbf{b}}(t,x,v)&\lesssim_{\delta,C_{E}}\min\{|n(x_{\mathbf{b}}(t,x,v))\cdot v_{\mathbf{b}}(t,x,v)|,\ 1\}.\end{split}

In addition, we have

\begin{split}|\nabla_{v}t_{\mathbf{b}}(t,x,v)|\lesssim 1,\qquad|\nabla_{v}x_{\mathbf{b}}(t,x,v)|\lesssim 1.\end{split}

Lemma 6
.

Fix some
t>0
. Assume that
\|\nabla_{x}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}t\ll 1
.
For any
\max\{0,t-t_{\mathbf{b}}\}\leq s\leq t
and
0<\theta\leq 1
,

(2.41)

\frac{e^{\theta|v|^{2}}}{e^{\theta|V(s;t,x,v)|^{2}}}\lesssim e^{o(1)(t-s)|v|}\lesssim e^{\frac{1}{2}\theta|v|^{2}}.

Similarly, for any
\max\{0,t-t_{\mathbf{b}}\}\leq s\leq t
,

(2.42)

\frac{\nu(v)}{\nu(V(s;t,x,v))}\lesssim 1.

Consequently, the following estimate holds:

\begin{split}&e^{-\int^{t}_{s}\frac{\nu(V(\tau))}{2}\mathrm{d}\tau}\frac{w_{\tilde{\theta}}(v)}{w_{\tilde{\theta}}(V(s;t,x,v))}\lesssim e^{-\int^{t}_{s}\frac{\nu(V(\tau))}{4}\mathrm{d}\tau}.\end{split}

Proof.

From the characteristic, for any
\max\{0,t-t_{\mathbf{b}}\}\leq s\leq t
,

(2.43)

\big||V(s;t,x,v)|-|v|\big|\leq\|\nabla_{x}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}t.

This implies that

\begin{split}\frac{e^{\theta|v|^{2}}}{e^{\theta|V(s;t,x,v)|^{2}}}&\lesssim\exp\big\{\theta\big(2\|\nabla_{x}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}|v|(t-s)+\|\nabla_{x}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}^{2}(t-s)^{2}\big)\big\}.\end{split}

Using the assumption that
\|\nabla_{x}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}t\ll 1
, we conclude (
2.41
).
Similarly, for any
\max\{0,t-t_{\mathbf{b}}\}\leq s\leq t
,

\frac{\nu(v)}{\nu(V(s;t,x,v))}\lesssim\frac{\nu(v)}{\nu(|v|-\|\nabla_{x}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}t)}\lesssim 1,

and thus we conclude (
2.42
).
∎

Lemma 7
.

Assume that
\|\nabla_{x}^{2}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}+\|\nabla_{x}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\ll 1
.
Fix some
t>0
.
Further, assume that
\|\nabla_{x}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}t\ll 1
.
For any
0<\theta\leq 1
and
\max\{0,t-t_{\mathbf{b}}\}\leq s\leq t
,

(2.44)

\displaystyle\big\|e^{-\theta|v|^{2}}\nabla^{2}_{v}X(s;t,x,v)\big\|_{L^{p}_{x,v}}

\displaystyle\lesssim|t-s|^{2}e^{\frac{3}{2}\|\nabla_{x}^{2}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}(t-s)^{2}}\int^{t}_{s}\int^{t}_{s_{1}}\big\|\nabla_{x}^{3}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\big\|_{L^{p}_{x}}\mathrm{d}s_{2}\mathrm{d}s_{1},

(2.45)

\displaystyle\big\|e^{-\theta|v|^{2}}\nabla_{x}\nabla_{v}X(s;t,x,v)\big\|_{L^{p}_{x,v}}

\displaystyle\lesssim|t-s|e^{\frac{3}{2}\|\nabla_{x}^{2}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}(t-s)^{2}}\int^{t}_{s}\int^{t}_{s_{1}}\big\|\nabla_{x}^{3}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\big\|_{L^{p}_{x}}\mathrm{d}s_{2}\mathrm{d}s_{1}.

Proof.

First, we prove (
2.44
).
Following the proof of Lemma
2
, the characteristics of (
2.1
) implies that

\begin{split}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}|\nabla^{2}_{v}X(s;t,x,v)|&\lesssim|\nabla^{2}_{v}V(s;t,x,v)|,\\
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}|\nabla^{2}_{v}V(s;t,x,v)|&\lesssim|\nabla_{x}^{3}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})(X(s;t,x,v))||\nabla_{v}X(s;t,x,v)|^{2}+\|\nabla_{x}^{2}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}|\nabla^{2}_{v}X(s;t,x,v)|.\end{split}

Since
\nabla^{2}_{v}X(t;t,x,v)=\nabla^{2}_{v}x=0
,
\nabla^{2}_{v}V(t;t,x,v)=\nabla^{2}_{v}v=0
. Then we obtain

(2.46)

\begin{split}|\nabla^{2}_{v}X(s;t,x,v)|&\lesssim\int^{t}_{s}|\nabla^{2}_{v}V(s_{1};t,x,v)|\mathrm{d}s_{1}\\
&\lesssim\int^{t}_{s}\int^{t}_{s_{1}}|\nabla_{x}^{3}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})(X(s_{2};t,x,v))||\nabla_{v}X(s_{2};t,x,v)|^{2}\mathrm{d}s_{2}\mathrm{d}s_{1}\\
&\quad+\int^{t}_{s}\int^{t}_{s_{1}}\|\nabla_{x}^{2}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}|\nabla^{2}_{v}X(s_{2};t,x,v)|\mathrm{d}s_{2}\mathrm{d}s_{1}.\end{split}

Using Fubini’s theorem, we derive

\begin{split}\eqref{eq1:X_vv_Lp}&\lesssim\int^{t}_{s}\int^{t}_{s_{1}}|\nabla_{x}^{3}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})(X(s_{2};t,x,v))||\nabla_{v}X(s_{2};t,x,v)|^{2}\mathrm{d}s_{2}\mathrm{d}s_{1}\\
&\quad+\int^{t}_{s}(s_{2}-s)\|\nabla_{x}^{2}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}|\nabla^{2}_{v}X(s_{2};t,x,v)|\mathrm{d}s_{2}.\end{split}

Then the Gronwall’s inequality implies that

\begin{split}&|\nabla^{2}_{v}X(s;t,x,v)|\\
&\lesssim\exp\big(\|\nabla_{x}^{2}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\int^{t}_{s}(s_{2}-s)\mathrm{d}s_{2}\big)\int^{t}_{s}\int^{t}_{s_{1}}|\nabla_{x}^{3}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})(X(s_{2};t,x,v))||\nabla_{v}X(s_{2};t,x,v)|^{2}\mathrm{d}s_{2}\mathrm{d}s_{1}\\
&\leq e^{\|\nabla_{x}^{2}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\frac{(t-s)^{2}}{2}}\int^{t}_{s}\int^{t}_{s_{1}}|\nabla_{x}^{3}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})(X(s_{2};t,x,v))||\nabla_{v}X(s_{2};t,x,v)|^{2}\mathrm{d}s_{2}\mathrm{d}s_{1}.\end{split}

This, together with (
2.3
), implies that

(2.47)

\begin{split}|\nabla^{2}_{v}X(s;t,x,v)|\lesssim|t-s|^{2}e^{\frac{3}{2}\|\nabla_{x}^{2}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}(t-s)^{2}}\int^{t}_{s}\int^{t}_{s_{1}}|\nabla_{x}^{3}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})(X(s_{2};t,x,v))|\mathrm{d}s_{2}\mathrm{d}s_{1}.\end{split}

Using (
2.41
) in Lemma
6
and Minkowski’s integral inequality, from (
2.47
) we derive that

\begin{split}&\big\|e^{-\theta|v|^{2}}\nabla^{2}_{v}X(s;t,x,v)\big\|_{L^{p}_{x,v}}\\
&\lesssim|t-s|^{2}e^{\frac{3}{2}\|\nabla_{x}^{2}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}(t-s)^{2}}\int^{t}_{s}\int^{t}_{s_{1}}\big\|e^{-\frac{1}{2}\theta|V(s_{2};t,x,v)|^{2}}\nabla_{x}^{3}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})(X(s_{2};t,x,v))\big\|_{L^{p}_{x,v}}\mathrm{d}s_{2}\mathrm{d}s_{1}.\end{split}

Since
\max\{0,t-t_{\mathbf{b}}\}\leq s\leq t
, the change of variable in Lemma
4
implies that

\begin{split}\big\|e^{-\theta|v|^{2}}\nabla^{2}_{v}X(s;t,x,v)\big\|_{L^{p}_{x,v}}\lesssim|t-s|^{2}e^{\frac{3}{2}\|\nabla_{x}^{2}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}(t-s)^{2}}\int^{t}_{s}\int^{t}_{s_{1}}\big\|\nabla_{x}^{3}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\big\|_{L^{p}_{x}}\mathrm{d}s_{2}\mathrm{d}s_{1}.\end{split}

Second, we prove (
2.45
).
Again from the proof of Lemma
2
, the characteristics of (
2.1
) implies that

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}|\nabla_{x}\nabla_{v}X(s;t,x,v)|\lesssim|\nabla_{x}\nabla_{v}V(s;t,x,v)|,

and thus,

\begin{split}&\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}|\nabla_{x}\nabla_{v}V(s;t,x,v)|\\
&\lesssim|\nabla^{3}_{x}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})||\nabla_{v}X(s;t,x,v)||\nabla_{x}X(s;t,x,v)|+|\nabla^{2}_{x}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})||\nabla_{x}\nabla_{v}X(s;t,x,v)|\\
&\leq|\nabla_{x}^{3}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})(X(s;t,x,v))||\nabla_{v}X(s;t,x,v)||\nabla_{x}X(s;t,x,v)|+\|\nabla_{x}^{2}(\phi_{f}+\phi_{E})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}|\nabla_{x}\nabla_{v}X(s;t,x,v)|.\end{split}

Since
